ParlaMint-IT_2019-02-06-LEG18-Senato-sed-88.ana.u4	The next item is the continuation of the debate on draft constitutional law Nos. 214, 515 and 805.
ParlaMint-IT_2019-02-06-LEG18-Senato-sed-88.ana.u5	Mr President, members of the Government, ladies and gentlemen, here we are again: the systematic work of demolition, scientifically designed by a few subjects, of the democratic system that we have lived up to this parliamentary term, which has been built hard and at the prices that we know well paid by our parents, with all the principles consecrated in our Constitution, by many considered the most beautiful in the world. Our Charter certainly had and needs to be developed, revised, but the fundamental cornerstones of our democracy, our democratic system, cannot be affected. The measure under discussion, on the other hand, is merely a confirmation of the intentions pursued by those who lead this devoutly silent majority. We had a clear signal at the time of the adoption of the budget law, which was laid down in the House after a period of exhausting delays and deepenings, approved by a majority which did not even have direct knowledge of the content. On the other hand, many other measures have been approved with the same system: packaged by the Government, delivered for the approval of the majority incapable of any comparison, with the only task of asserting the force of numbers, private as it is of the faculty to discuss the orders proposed from above; little professionalism, scarce skills, with the ill-concealed will to arid management of power, masked by the alleged need to comply with the popular will. This is yet another demagogic operation, with at least a questionable measure, which will bring disastrous effects to the detriment of the fundamental principles of our democracy. The people call for a reduction in the number of parliamentarians - this is true - who make up the caste they earn so much to produce nothing, who think only of themselves and never of the common good: this is the imagination that has been consciously fed. Here this majority responds once again to the belly of the people and puts on a measure that apodittically cuts the number of parliamentarians with a blow of machete, settling a very hard blow to our parliamentary system based essentially on the principle of representation, which ensures the democratic management of our country. The intention is now dramatically clear, although it had already been openly declined by the summits of that silent majority: the aim is to dismantle Parliament, the institution which represents the highest expression of the democratic principles on which our Republic is founded, since it is made up of representatives of the people who elected them to carry out that function. The anti-parliamentaryism which animates the measure under consideration is based precisely on the desire to achieve the objective of completely demolishing the parliamentary system, the democratic parliamentary system which - as I said - is the place of the greatest expression of democracy. This government therefore gives vent to populism and demagogy and proposes a measure to be used in the next election campaign for the European elections. He does so by appealing also to another laughable argument, that of the containment of the expenditures of politics. This is another demagogic issue because, beyond the figures that this majority is filling its mouth with, saying that it will save the considerable figure of about EUR 40 million, it fails to say that the costs of the institution of Parliament are many others. Parliament is a complex institution, as it is normal for it to be an institution which is the garrison of democracy, which - as is well known - has high costs. That of Members, however, is very little compared to the cost - indeed high - of Parliament's structures that will remain unchanged, will not be affected and are more than ten times higher than would be saved by the cut-off of Members proposed by the measure under consideration. The Democratic Party and I myself - as is well known - were not and are not opposed to the reduction in the number of parliamentarians, but only on condition that the fundamental values of the system and, in particular, the principle of representation are not affected. It is therefore clear that it is necessary to put hand to the fundamental anomaly of our parliamentary system, based on equal bicameralism, which is now anachronistic and an objective reason for slowing down parliamentary activity. In this respect, the amendments tabled by the Democratic Party, which, while providing for a reduction in the number of Members of Parliament, also include the revision of the bicameralism of equality, while at the same time allowing the principle of representation to be safeguarded. I am sure that the majority's attitude towards these amendments will be significant in revealing the true objectives of the reform. It is undeniable that the reduction in the number of Members raises a number of problems. Let us start by saying that the number of our Members tends to be in line with that of the parliaments of the rest of Europe and, indeed, in the United States, we are even trying to increase it. In Europe there are very few, mostly far-right tendencies, and the reduction is being made considering that the representatives of the people should not affect the democratic life of the country. I think it is pointless to recall that the prime idea of reducing parliamentarians was sadly known P2: the programme was to reduce the number of representatives, and this was done in such a way that it would be easier to control Parliament by political secretariats, leading, precisely, to the formation of a Parliament made up of sole directors, thus preventing any extra-party opportunities and experience. This is in clear antinomy with the fundamental principles of political formation which has gained greater consensus in the last parliamentary elections and which is now sitting in Parliament. Yet, they are approving this sad reform. On the other hand, the reduction proposed today would create serious problems of representation of the small Regions: Valle d'Aosta, Umbria, Molise, Basilicata and Sardinia, my Region. These regions, in fact, would run the serious risk of not seeing any candidate elected, especially to the Senate. On the contrary, let's say that from the facts, considering that the candidates are elected on a regional basis, Sardinia certainly would not have any representative in the Senate, taking into account the small number of parliamentarians: only the elected of the Regions with the highest number of inhabitants would be part of this Assembly. This would be the case with the situation that Sardinia already lives with the European elections: having a college combined with Sicily, you can never or almost never - unless you can find specific agreements - send a representative of our island to Europe. In this sense I appeal to my colleague, Mr Solinas, who is a candidate for the presidency of the Region - and I am sorry that he is not here today - to ensure, however, that it is also because of the fact that he is here representing the Sardinian people, that he protects the need to maintain the democratic system. I would like to address my Sardinian colleagues in the Movimento 5 Stars sitting in this House, who remain silent and fail to make their voices heard, although they are aware that the effects of this reform will be those I have just described, and we will see that. Above all they are unable to oppose the destruction of the democratic system with which - thank God - we have managed to reach today. They remain silent because the orders, once again, come from above. In this way, all we do is confirm what the real objectives of the reform are. On the other hand, with regard to the destruction of the parliamentary system, some of the big ones - let me say this - have already pointed out that Parliament is absolutely useless. We are not talking about the conduct of parliamentary activities and, in particular, about the functioning of the Commissions. Have you raised the question of how the Commissions can work? Even today we have difficulties in making them work in a dignified way; imagine what will happen when there are 200 parliamentarians in this Senate, or those who will be. How will the work be carried out in the Commissions? Each Member of Parliament will have to be a member of at least five, six, seven Commissions. We are moving towards the paralysis of Parliament, which is the objective of the reform in fact. It is therefore clear that the reduction in the number of senators and Members, as designed by this measure, if not accompanied by a revision of the bicameral system, would only lead to obstacles to the smooth running of parliamentary activity. The king would be naked; one would understand the real intent pursued by this Government, that is, a progressive break-up of Parliament, of the democratic system, until it was eliminated to allow few to govern the country. The address is now clear, but the Democratic Party will strongly oppose this design: it will oppose the violation of the fundamental values of our democratic system. [[Applause from the PD Group and Senator Fantetti]]
ParlaMint-IT_2019-02-06-LEG18-Senato-sed-88.ana.u6	Senator Maiorino is registered to speak. You have the right to do so.
ParlaMint-IT_2019-02-06-LEG18-Senato-sed-88.ana.u7	Mr President, ladies and gentlemen, let me just read you the following words for a moment: It is necessary to start with the drastic reduction in the number of Members: 400 Members and 200 Senators. In this way it will be easier to organize the work of the Chambers and it will become more efficient the process of approval of the laws, without affecting in any way the supreme principle of representation........................................................................ These words come from the government contract, and we, today, are in the Assembly for the first vote on the bill which translates those words into deeds, confirming that we, if we say so, do so. It took the Movimento 5 Stars and it required this Government, supported by this majority - the first in the history of the Republic to be born on the basis of a real government contract, written black on white and in full transparency - to bring to the Chamber and Senate Assemblies an overall plan of punctual proposals for revision of the Constitution. Take good care, though: these are not the usual plans to dismantle the fundamental Charter - as the old left and right parties have done - but slender and targeted measures. The measure under consideration only provides for a cut in the number of Members, so that it will be easy for people to decide whether to vote yes or no. It took the 5 Star Movimento to the Government to realize not only the points of the contract, but our dreams, the dreams of so many people who have been waiting for years answers from politics. This, today, is certainly the realization of a dream: to cut the number of parliamentarians. How many years have we been talking about this at banquets, meetings and squares? From 1985 to the present day, seven attempts have been repeated, all stranded in nothingness. Why is it necessary to cut the number of Members? For a mere question of savings? The savings are certainly not small, as we are talking about about about EUR 500 million per legislature and EUR 300,000 per day. But is it really just for accounting that we want it? I'd say no, there's a lot more. The reduction in the number of Members will contribute to greater efficiency in the functioning of the Chambers and, contrary to what the opponents continue to support, the measure under consideration will make the proportion between the number of elected and the number of voters more balanced. In fact, I would like to remind you that almost everywhere the numbers are quite different from ours: for example, there are 650 MPs elected in the United Kingdom, in Spain there are 558, in France 577, in Germany about 700. Therefore, this argument is absolutely unfounded. There is, however, a third reason behind the genesis of this draft constitutional revision, which in my opinion is the most important one. In this House we are legislators, but we in the Movimento 5 Stars are also spokesmen, which means that we directly collect citizens' demands. This measure responds to a deep need expressed by civil society, aimed at seizing the gap between politics and citizens. [[Applause from the M5S Group]] The measure under consideration is in fact only a part of a broader and more ambitious framework of political and regulatory actions aimed at restoring a relationship of trust between Italians and institutions; a relationship that has worn out over the decades. The deepest rift between the political palaces and Italy, which toil every day with dignity, took place with the scandal of Tangentopoli in 1992. Since then, however, he has not sewn up. He certainly did not sew himself up with that operetta policy that we saw during the Second Republic. In fact, mistrust and disaffection towards republican institutions have become chronic, leading many citizens to disamoration and separation from political commitment. One of our highest ambitions is to give citizens a new interest in politics and the desire to participate; this can only be achieved by showing that we are not selfish, that we do not hide behind fictitious objections of diminished representativeness, to conceal the fact that politicians are not willing to make sacrifices and also to put their position at risk, if this is in the interests of the community. We are not here to do our own interests or those of the privileged few. We're here to do the real interests of the country. Rethinking the number of Members therefore represents a step in this direction and is part of a broader action, which includes the reduction of the vitality of former Members, already carried out [[Applause from the M5S Group]] ; the reduction of the salaries of the MPs in office, on which we will work very soon; the introduction of the proposed referendum and many other actions which aim - on the one hand - to restore confidence in politics with capital P and, on the other hand, to encourage the active participation of citizens in the decision-making and legislative process; sober and efficient representative democracy, which one step at a time prepares the ground for direct democracy, where all citizens can participate without delegate others. This is how we want to reconstruct the sense of belonging to the Constitution and the institutions. The tools are there and the times are ripe. Direct democracy is a necessary way forward; it is the necessary development to gather the message that comes loud and clear not only from Italy, but from the whole of Europe: the citizens ask us to participate and to be able to do so, to decide on their future. These are historical battles of the Movimento 5 Stars, but not by whim and not by populism, now abused expression with which you label anything different from the usual recipes of a faded and tired partyism. No, they are battles in which we believe, because only through the reconstruction of the lost trust can social cohesion, which is so necessary in a true democracy, be re-founded. What the citizens are asking of politics counts, is heard and understood: today we show, once again, and do so precisely in the supreme name of democracy; a word that for too long has sounded empty to the ears of the citizens and to which we, with pride, want to give back weight, meaning and value. [[Applause from the M5S Group and Senator Pirovano]] A final appeal to colleagues in the opposition: all your parties have made proposals over time to reduce the number of MPs and all your parties have failed to complete them. [[Comments of Senator Malpezzi]] Today you have the opportunity to show that you are serious and to take a step forward towards the Italians who look at us with renewed hope. [[Applause from the M5S Group and Senator Pirovano. Comments from the PD Group]]
ParlaMint-IT_2019-02-06-LEG18-Senato-sed-88.ana.u8	Senator D'Arienzo is enlisted to speak. You have the right to do so.
ParlaMint-IT_2019-02-06-LEG18-Senato-sed-88.ana.u9	Mr President, listening to the previous speech, I can think of what I sometimes say to my children: it is as if the world was born the day before yesterday; they arrived and there was nothing else before. It's really sickening. [[Applause from the PD Group]] On the other hand, the draft law before us, my dear colleague, has an important limit, because it deals with reform not as a whole, but only a small piece of it, a part of it, that of the reduction of parliamentarians, as if the obstacle to our democracy and the efficiency of the institutions were the number of those who sit in the parliamentary classrooms. However, as is well known, although it is pompously presented as the panacea of all evils, the proposal does not really affect our electoral system of 2017, neither the proportions, nor the colleges, nor the allocation of parliamentarians. So - to put it briefly, ladies and gentlemen - it seems to me that the present majority, particularly those who have opposed that reform in the past, the much-vilified Rosatellum, is more than well. Not the protests of the past! With this proposal you change only the numbers that that system produces, but not the mechanism. I believe, therefore, that it is a legislative artifice in order not to pursue the true reforms necessary to change our country. Imagine or even claim - as you do with this proposal - that your criterion of determining the number of parliamentarians is perennially applicable, regardless of the number fixed by the Constitution and, above all, without the necessary changes to adapt this system to the electoral law, the country and the democratic moment in which you live, means living on the moon. No electoral system lives perennially, for obvious reasons; it proves that it is the last years of our political life. To further illuminate the limits of this proposal, there is also the fact that it does not modify the districts: this strengthens our conviction that the process you have begun is absolutely inadequate. And I am strengthened in this awareness - I listened to it just as I did in the past - for two reasons: first of all we talk only about money. The Italian democracy of decisions has become an economic ornament and someone does nothing but blabulate - as has been done even a while ago - on the savings that would result: this is the first conviction. The second conviction - I say this to my colleague who mentioned only absolute numbers - is that our number of parliamentarians, compared to those of other European countries, is rather low: we take over the twentieth place in the ranking. It is not the total number that makes the difference, but it is the relationship between that number and the voters. In Italy we have an average of 63,644 voters per Member of Parliament, while the European average is 46,515: 20,000 fewer voters. When reference is made to the absolute number, a mistake is made which is the result of that negative analysis which led to the proposal being examined. The debate is not new - you are right - because seven times in the past we have tried to change the number of Members, but never disconnected it from everything else, that is from all the other reforms that would be needed to adapt our democracy to the current needs. Now we try again, but there is no debate in the country, there is no bicameral Commission and not even a parliamentary debate or a full-bodied reform: there is only the accounting aspect. And the source of this change is not, precisely, the answer to a concrete requirement that exists in the country and in the institutions - we experienced a referendum a few months ago in this regard - but it is a principle established in what they call a government contract and which is repeatedly mentioned as the Bible. It will also be true that the current government is the first government that is born with a contract, but we hope it is also the last one, if these are the results! Therefore, on the basis of that very authoritative source of life and law, it is believed that the speed of Parliament's work is linked to the number of Members: a nonsense, a conceptual nonsense of departure that derails everything else, until the final choice you have reached. I can reasonally say, based on what you are doing in these months, that money does not set reforms; indeed, a man of common sense would never have approved slot measures like yours on citizenship income. You say that the number of Members causes costs, you raise the problem of reducing the costs of politics and then you spend billions of euros to finance nothing. That the proposal has the short breath of those who have decided with the calculator will be understood very well at the time when it will have to be implemented. The current number of parliamentarians guarantees a proportional balance between large and small regions: with this proposal you will break that balance; on the contrary, you will reduce the smaller regions to no representation, which is the height of the peaks. What about the fact that we will have gigantic electoral colleges, which will have distorting effects? The first is certainly the direct relationship between the candidate and the voters. Many times we have said that the voter must choose the candidate he recognizes and knows, rewarding precisely the best. In a boarding school like that, I want to see how they're gonna pick the best. The second aspect is equal opportunities: it is obvious that in a much larger college, such as the one you are producing with this reform, the one with more resources will be stronger than those who do not have it. You will, therefore, reward those who have the most and harm those who have the least; and this, all the more so, by virtue of the fact that - as is known - from 2000 to 2017 voters have grown by two million people. This aspect, as far as we are concerned, is very critical. In essence, this proposal is untied by a genuine institutional and constitutional reform, which depresses the representation of the smaller territories, benefits the rich, does not bring benefits in terms of efficiency in parliamentary work and does not improve the electoral-elected relationship which - in my view - is already good. What is your purpose and purpose in this reform? The first is the mass distraction: to bring back the reduction of parliamentarians in view, moreover, of the next electoral campaign for Europeans, to distract Italians from your poor economic results and the real scope of measures such as 100 quota or citizenship income, from your firm opposition to investments for the growth and development of the country and from the difficulties that you can no longer support. [[Applause from the PD Group]] This is only the best opportunity to seek a cross-based appreciation among voters, a weapon of distraction, in fact. This is the usual argument you use, because it is good for all seasons: it was good yesterday, it is good today and it will be good tomorrow. But it is only the slice of ham that you put before the eyes of the Italians because you will not solve any problem with this proposal. Those who think that the problem is reforming our democracy of decisions through these reforms are wrong; that is not what is needed. The theme is, in fact, what democracy we want for the future of our country and how we want it to guarantee the basic principle of every democracy, the governance, efficiency and effectiveness of decision-making organisations. And this decision will always be faster and more efficient the more and sooner we will overcome - in my view - the perfect and equal bicameralism, but always keeping the pillars of representative democracy firm. That of direct democracy that goes through the reduction of Members is just a lie, because it will change absolutely nothing compared to that objective. I should like to conclude by saying that the proposal - as I have already pointed out - does not deal with the real problem, namely the overcoming of the perfect and equal bicameralism. Of course, there has been a referendum which obviously cannot be ignored. However, it is precisely because of that referendum that it would have been useful to start a debate in the country, everywhere, to reflect on what are however the opportunities for debate between us, to see what to improve and what to take from that referendum, which obviously cannot be overcome. [[Applause from the PD Group]] There is nothing to take away, however, that the fact that you only point to that aspect makes reform short-sighted and not adequate for what is needed. As a result, Mr President, any other road other than that main objective is a waste of time. [[Applause from the PD Group. Congratulations.]]
ParlaMint-IT_2019-02-06-LEG18-Senato-sed-88.ana.u10	Senator Berutti is enlisted to speak. You have the right to do so.
ParlaMint-IT_2019-02-06-LEG18-Senato-sed-88.ana.u11	Mr President, ladies and gentlemen, the story that we are lucky enough to breathe every time we are going to perform an important part of the tasks entrusted to us in this House could lead us to the gross mistake of thinking that the Senate, Parliament and, more generally, the institutions are always equal to themselves. In truth, certainly and fortunately, the institutions stand out in order to be subject to a continuous evolution that is declined in the sign of continuity, and this happens in a system so to speak dual, in which the component of evolution involves continuous changes in the characteristics and structure embodied by the institutions themselves to be as functional as possible to their main objective: to be at the service of the citizens. The institutional changes that have characterized the history of our country are difficult to synthesize. They inevitably dealt with the many aspects on which the institutions are based, enabling them to function. If we look at this multiplicity of changes or possibilities for change, there are two characteristics that emerge clearly: complexity and, in it, the continuing propensity to adapt the institutions and among them, first and foremost, parliamentary ones, to the outside world, to the country and its demands. Management of complexity and willingness to adapt institutions to the characteristics and needs of the country are two issues that Forza Italia has no need to learn from anyone. History testifies to that. On the front of the desire to adapt the institutions to the country suffice to recall and reiterate that, as early as 2005, with the approval of the constitutional bill of amendments to Part II of the Constitution, Forza Italia had intervened, approving also a significant reduction in the number of parliamentarians. For complex reasons, which I will not retrace, the proposal was not confirmed in the referendary; however, it is undeniable that we were also at the forefront on that front. So, as I said, the proposal to reduce the number of parliamentarians contained in our 2005 draft Constitutional Law was one of the ambitious elements, and to a good extent still current, of a much broader intervention that had to do with the way in which the country and its institutions understood it. This brings us to the other element which I have mentioned as characteristic of the speech on the institutions: the management of complexity, a capacity which the contents of our 2005 text bear witness to, given that there were together interventions on the front of the actors called to compose the Chambers, on the environmental or structural one of Parliament, or on its mode of operation. I do not go further in this historical analysis, because it is evident that in perspective the contribution of Forza Italia on these matches has always been virtuous and consistent. I'm coming to the present. We have often heard in these days, and we will hear it again, that the reduction in the number of Members of Parliament is contained in the Government contract. All right, good. Before your contract, however, the reduction in the number of parliamentarians is contained in the programme backed tirelessly by Forza Italia for the elections that have given rise to this Parliament. [[Applause from the FI-BP Group]] In our programme, however, that reference is accompanied by the reference to other important interventions on the institutional front: from the direct election of the President of the Republic to the strengthening of the autonomy of the intermediate and local authorities. We are once again faced with the issue of managing complexity. A complexity that Mr Quagliarello does not forget to reiterate in his draft law, reminding us that, however, it is increasingly urgent to avoid the need to resort to easy alibis, such as the one on the electoral law, on which to discharge all the responsibility of an unstable system. This premise, as I said, follows the observation of the need to keep together in the reasoning the awareness that a broader reform is necessary which respectfully invests Parliament and, above all, the form of government. At this point, however, the proposal from the Forza Italia files combines a broad assessment with a consideration of sound political realism, proposing to intervene with a reduction in the number of parliamentarians, but also considering the opportunity to rationalise the timing of the bicameral system and the functioning of the legislative procedure. The reduction in the number of parliamentarians is one of the elements of a broader and more comprehensive vision of the institutions and their way of serving the country. So let me say that the conscious view expressed by Mr Quagliarello in his proposal does not emerge from the texts put forward on this subject by the majority, and that is clearly a problem. It is clear to everyone that in some ways it is time for the policy to be reduced, and the intervention on Articles 56 and 57 of the Constitution is a possible step in this direction. The issue, however, is how that step is taken. As I have already said, the proposal born between the benches of Forza Italia includes the reduction of parliamentarians as one of the elements of a plan that is virtuously complex and that in its decline would protect and protect the functions and dignity of Parliament, representation, weights and counterweights indispensable in a democratic system. The majority's draft legislation and its proposals, on the other hand, appeared and appear to be reductionist; so much reductionist that even the rapporteur has decided to reduce, rather than cancel, his contribution to this House. I would like to say a few words on this point. We are, once again, faced with an intervention which, as presented, appears to be a permanent campaign commercial, which does not look at the complexity, which does not look at the inlay between the constitutional arrangement and the political system, the electoral system, the Regulations, the internal functioning of the Chambers and so on. But basically this is what you can do: good commercials for social networks, which can be summarized with a few words of impact without thinking about the consequences and complexity. The risk, removed the complexity, is that, instead of putting in place an intervention that helps to adapt, as right, the institutions to the country, is merely propaganda. The deepest fear, however, is that an intervention designed in the terms in which you have thought of it is included in a broader and more fraudulent project, which aims to attack the functions and dignity of Parliament. That Parliament that your mentor would like to turn into a Parliament of drawers. [[Applause from the FI-BP Group]] On this front, as in the case of the monstrous referendum without quorum that you insist on proposing, sooner or later - I hope more sooner than later - you will have to explain to us what your aversion to drivers and your natural propensity to take it out on those who should be responsible. But you don't know what the responsibility is, because you put everything back to the technicians, the cost-benefit analyses and the so-called hands. On the contrary, politics is the opposite of your continuing flight from reality and of your willingness to take it - as I said - with those who should have the responsibility to lead the country towards better growth. It is obvious - as the history of Forza Italia says - that for us the goal is to start a path that makes politics closer, agile, effective, efficient and useful to those who work and create work. Forza Italia will not back down. But know that our favor is not based on the same reasons that some algorithm has thought for you. [[Applause from the FI-BP Group]]
ParlaMint-IT_2019-02-06-LEG18-Senato-sed-88.ana.u12	He's enlisted to speak Senator Stephen. You have the right to do so.
ParlaMint-IT_2019-02-06-LEG18-Senato-sed-88.ana.u13	Mr President, ladies and gentlemen, senators, representatives of the government, the rank of constitutional law that concerns the bill under consideration today in the Senate calls us to be as free as possible from any faction and to place ourselves above any outrage (sine ira et studio, someone would say). At first glance, the measure would seem to facilitate our task, as it consists of a short articulation, so much - perhaps too much - dry and with few numerical modifications. This text seems to ratify the triumph of the deductive process, in spite of the plurality and the different arguments contained in the different texts related to the draft law under consideration. This numerical reduction hides - instead - a complex and articulated matter. I am thinking, for example, of the representation of our political system and of the representativeness of the elected, of the role and weight of the representative assemblies, and of the importance of our representative democracy. I have repeated the concepts of representation and representativeness several times, because the measure under consideration, which reduces the number of senators and deputies, cannot be associated - as it is formally - with the Act of the Senate 881, concerning the applicability of electoral laws regardless of the number of parliamentarians. I'll explain. You cannot liquidate the measure as a simple size-seater, as in the graphic postcard that some colleague of the Movimento 5 Stars, or rather, the city spokesperson has already shown on social media. The real arrangement that accompanies the bill under consideration is the vision of Davide Casaleggio, who bullies our representative democracy as a fetish that will soon be overcome. [[Applause from the PD Group]] Then there is the statement by Alessandro Di Battista, deputy shadow premier, who judges our democratic system obsolete as the monarchy. It is therefore with the law of the Associate House that you must read and understand the deep sense of your initiative and the hidden design that this arrangement traces. At the end of the day, Minister Fraccaro's words last June were sibile in announcing that the institutional updating measures would be adopted through separate and autonomous constitutional initiatives, thus giving up a comprehensive and comprehensive reform programme. It would therefore be appropriate for the Government to clarify more clearly your concept - as a majority - of institutional updates. We must remember that our Constitution is not a software produced in Cupertino, which is updated automatically from time to time. Our Constitution is much more than your Rousseau platform. [[Applause from the PD Group]] However, today you are bringing the number of Members to 400 and the number of senators to 200 to the House. I wonder: can this - and this is right - be the point of fall in the path of innovation of the top institutions of our system? Are you sure that it is not yet necessary to intervene first on the characteristics of our undifferentiated bicameralism? Are you really convinced that you will still be able to bluff the citizens by saying that with this reduction you have reformed Parliament? I personally believe that the answer to all these questions is and will be: no. I say more: what I am criticizing today in this House is the approach to representative democracy as if it were a simple voice of current spending and the desire to feed the anti-politics, with which you have so far inflated your percentages with the instruments proper to democratic representation. Nor do I have any difficulty in declaring here in this House that at first I had general concerns about this draft law that, through a serene debate and a targeted amending activity, I thought could also find an easy solution. In the Senate, in fact, access thresholds could exceed 10% and this percentage is the same maximum measure tolerated by the European Court of Human Rights in proportional systems. ParlaMint-IT_2019-02-06-LEG18-Senato-sed-88.ana.u15	Mr President, ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen, it is a particular moment that we are living in this House today because the constitutional reform measure that we have been waiting for very long has finally arrived and on some points we believe there may be a sharing. I will divide my speech in two moments, one "hot" and one "cold." The first part inevitably from the silence of the rapporteur: he came to the Chamber without explaining the reasons why this measure can be founded and I do not believe that this is due solely to the intention to save time, as I will say at the end of my speech. In fact, I believe that his elusive words, in fact, are very explanatory to the feelings and contrasts that this majority lives. The intention is probably to have the measure passed quickly, without a hit and without a big debate, and I must say that I absolutely do not agree with that. This morning I listened very carefully to the speeches made by the Senators of the majority and the Movimento 5 Stars: perhaps, colleagues, you talked about these constitutional issues in banquets, social media and other suitable environments. I remember, however, that in 1979, when, at the age of nineteen - for me it was the time of the university - the professors, held their concisions in class, let us discuss and the discussions continued even after, in all subjects. We were talking about presidential reform and all aspects of the Constitution, but I have to say frankly that Articles 56 and 57 did not attract so much attention from us students, because the Constitution, although it is a "living" matter, is also a "cold" matter and therefore hardly excites. At the beginning of the 1980s, however, there was a great attempt at constitutional reform: there was then an attempt to lead to a new Republic, particularly of a presidential nature, which we would still like to support in this House. Reforms cannot be done simply by the sound of a reduction in the number of Members of Parliament. We would have expected something different and we would have liked a reform of the entire state system: we tried in previous legislation, because for forty years there has been an attempt to change the institutional system to make the legislative system more fluid; there are many governments that have tried to do this reform, but not simply to reduce a number, but considering an overall system. I still remember Berlusconi in the 14th legislature, but I could go back: the number that is indicated today is essentially in line with that indicated by the Commission De Mita-Iotti, i.e. 200 senators and 400 Members, is not that it is a great novelty. I must also say that the failures that have occurred cannot be compensated simply by the presentation and the obtaining of a vote in favour that we may be able to register in this House. My impression is that with the European elections approaching, someone wants to go to the streets and flag a result. Actually, if we don't think about cum wheat salt, we risk throwing the baby away with dirty water. So let us be very careful: we can reduce the number of Members of Parliament, let alone agree, so much so that in the 14th legislature we ourselves were the advocates of a bill in this respect, but today we are simply inside this niche and it seems to me frankly a little bit less. So if anyone wants to flag this attempt to reform our Constitutional Charter in the next election, I have to say that the fascination process does not involve us completely: we would have wanted something different. We naturally accept the reduction because it is an important point of reference, as I said, but the new face of the State does not go through this simple measure. I've seen that someone has already used aural social themes, very strong ways of expressing themselves, but I think that in this case the mountain has given birth to the mouse. Well, we can stand on the side of the mouse, we can give legitimacy and recognize that there are good reasons to support the reduction in the number of Members, but this reform had to be accompanied by something more serious. Of course, the reduction of expenditure is a good thing, but that cannot be the only reason why we should vote in favour or perhaps abstain on this measure. Action must also be reduced and rationalised in order to facilitate the legislative process. I believe that this establishment of the Constitution is flawed in many other respects, in many other respects. We are not, in essence, able to legislate correctly for a very simple reason relating to the various passages that must pass through a measure, which enters the Commission written in a certain way, is modified, after which it faces the dual passage of Chamber and Senate and, finally, if it should ever be passed as a law, it could be disenforced in some cases by the judiciary and in some other cases challenged before the Constitutional Court. I therefore believe that we should look very carefully at these aspects as well. We recognize that this measure was in the Government contract - let alone - but it was even earlier, as we had indicated, in the ropes and in the heart of Forza Italia, it was in the ropes and in the heart of those who wanted and want an organic reform of the State. These are the reasons. This measure does not serve to poison someone's low instincts, it does not serve to go to the electoral campaign to do anything else, to pin down a badge that, in fact, belongs to others. The most true testimony we can see is that when a political movement enters into crisis it immediately throws itself headlong in an attempt to reduce expenditure and to lower the number of parliamentarians. What has Macron done in France these days in the face of the crisis that has struck him? The first answer he gave was to reduce the number of Members. Well, that's the hot thing about this. I would not like the measure before our examination, in fact, to be the fig leaf on the entrepreneurial nudity represented by this Government, on its contradictions on the TAV, on the TAP, on the foreign policy positions on Venezuela. There are obvious contradictions and something had to mask this situation. These are the reasons why I believe that the measure we are considering has arrived in this House at the moment. In conclusion, I would like to point out that maintaining this system will hardly succeed, even with the reduction of Members, in achieving a complete reform. In fact, we should rather think about the differentiation of competences. I wonder if 200 senators will be enough to cover all the work of the Commissions. I still wonder whether it is not appropriate to envisage a differentiation in the powers of the Chambers. These are the aspects, which are also critical, which we allow ourselves to point out to this House, although of course we are in favour of rationalizing expenditure and reducing the number of Members. Beppe Grillo, with regard to the draw of parliamentarians, said that the greatest deception of politics is to make us believe that politicians are needed; it seems absurd, but think about it for a moment: the selection (that of the draw) should be fair and representative of the country, it would be a microcosm of society. 10:34 a.m. (Follows GRIMANI). As I said before, this theme does not frighten us. In the past years we have been interpreters of attempts at reform. Over the last thirty years, Parliament has been the protagonist of several attempts: there have been about seven, all of which have been empty. We therefore have no difficulty in talking about these issues; moreover, the history of the Italian left has been characterized by very strong currents of thought regarding the issue of institutional reform. I watched a book, which has been reprinted in recent years, written in 1985 by the President of the Ingrao Chamber (President in the years 1976 to 1979) with Norberto Bobbio precisely on the crisis of Parliament. In that text (we are already talking about 1985) there was the theme of institutional reform, of a Parliament which was losing its capacity to be the centre of representative democracy, which was in a phase of decay of public ethics; yet already in those years there was the issue of institutional reform, the reduction of the number of parliamentarians, the strengthening of Parliament's powers on the control side, the greater link between Parliament's institutions and European and regional institutions. So the issue of reform is central to us, but it is not only about reducing the running costs of the institutions. Using terms of comparison of this nature is too small and we who are the ruling class (and even more so you who are in the government today) have to ask ourselves the question of how institutions must function better and how they respond to the needs and questions of citizens. I believe that representative democracy is not out of date, and I consider it a mistake to think of it as a blur or a worn-out instrument. Representation is the backbone of modern democracies, where the need to compose spaces of social freedom prevails. This is the task of the political parties: the Constitution provides for this in Article 49, which must not be cleared. I address above all to the Senators of the Movimento 5 Stars: instead of bringing out your contradictions with regard to being a party or not, you prefer to dump this doubt on the institutions and not face it for what it really is. And then you think that the structure of democracy must be directed, without mediation, without synthesis or cohesion. If, on the other hand, you really want to interpret the mission of change - which is so much praised - you have to set a higher objective, that is to think of a more complete institutional reform, and then treat issues such as the balance between the legislative and the executive power; equal bicameralism; the excessive use of the decree of urgency, and the need to perhaps rethink this branch of Parliament as a Chamber of autonomy and guarantees. In this sense we had started a reform process, rejected by the voters - we would miss it, the real country decides - and you at that stage, where these issues were put to the attention of Parliament and the citizens, preferred to conduct a political battle against those who proposed that reform rather than looking at the general interest contemplated in the contents of the reform. That is why, in my opinion, Parliament is now facing this transition with maturity. The continuous denigration of the institutions leads nowhere, but only to the death of democracy. Today you who are in the government can no longer play the battle against the establishment: it is you establishment. [[Applause from the PD Group]] This battle no longer holds up; you must have the responsibility to deal with the issues because you are a force of government, and this task is yours because the voters have attributed it to you. ParlaMint-IT_2019-02-06-LEG18-Senato-sed-88.ana.u20	Mr President, Mr Undersecretary, ladies and gentlemen, constitutional reforms are an important moment in the life of Parliament and in political life in general. The current Constitution has already imposed complex limits and procedures, with weights and balances, and it has done so deliberately, given the object and influence that constitutional reforms can have on the functioning system of all politics and the state. There are balances that are - it is true - affected by the politics of the moment, but others that go even beyond the level of constitutional doctrine, which, by its nature, is slower and deepens the processes. The reduction in the number of parliamentarians, as has already been said, has been under discussion for years and corresponds to a common feeling of public opinion, urged by the press and the talk shows and perhaps it is also a fashion, so today those who oppose it would be condemned to ostracism. However, constitutionalists who have been audited by the 1st Commission and broadly agree on the reform raise a number of relevant objections. According to constitutionalists, the electoral process has a pre-election phase (the electoral system, the selection of nominations and the electoral campaign), a real electoral phase (the exercise of the vote and the election of representatives) and then a post-election phase (the report and the summary to voters by the elected). Depending on the prevalence at each stage of these meters of judgment and these methods can prevail systems more centered on the party or on the candidate from which it derives, once elected, the responsibility towards the voters: that of collecting the demands of the electorate and the party, exercising without mandate (I hope it is not one of the next reforms scheduled, because the absence of a bond of mandate guarantees the freedom of the elected) and then account for the activity carried out. In the system that is proposed and that will probably be approved there will be 200 senators, three octaves of which elected according to the single system: 60 million Italians about divided by 75 senators of the union correspond to about 800,000 inhabitants. With enormous proportional colleges, which correspond to whole regions or at least half of the region, it is a situation that is changing and that is difficult to sustain, because the further increase to 800,000 inhabitants also results in an increase in direct and indirect costs, beyond the budget, of the electoral campaign. It also changes the type of vote, because sometimes it affects more regions and certainly more provinces, so it changes the mindset and the type of vote of exchange, not in the criminalistic and forcaiola meaning that has been mentioned sometimes in this Assembly, but in the sense of a promise and a way of achieving something, as well as changing the vote of opinion or political belonging. In addition, the relationship between voters, elected and parties changes, because 800,000 inhabitants distort and can also make the elected one more independent. As far as the electoral system is concerned - but it is a provocation what I am saying - probably with the dry unitary the reference would be to 300,000 inhabitants (60,000 million divided by 200 senators), which is still a dimension to measure of the electoral man and woman, so the elected one can gradually know his college. However, constitutionalists wonder whether reducing the number of parliamentarians without changing the perfect bicameralism will give more efficiency and more political weight to the elected and taxpayers' voters. My speech will end with a voice of hope, despite everything. Although at this moment we cannot talk about changing the perfect bicameralism, because when we tried to do so we ended up badly, I believe that, while a smaller number of senators on the level of efficiency may perhaps lead to some difficulties in the management of the Commissions - but perhaps they will be grouped - will give the elected one greater political weight. Then there are some minor issues that are raised by the constitutionalists, including, first of all, that of the 58 regional delegates for the election of the President of the Republic who would have a different weight on the total number of those entitled to vote. Then there is the question of the active and passive electorate according to the constitutionalists: according to some schools, twenty-five years to vote in the Senate are too many, as well as forty to be elected. Personally, I think they are fine, because maturity is reached even further. The reform also provides for a maximum number of five senators for life, which I believe is the maximum that can be set, because otherwise the total of 200 could become a particular group, which can affect the results. In conclusion, moving from constitutional law, which is a bit difficult for me, to reality, what is the figure, what is the function and what are the powers of the Member of Parliament? Who's he answering? Does a Member of Parliament today answer the people of his constituency, the voters, the taxpayers, all or only those of his condominium and those who have expressed a certain type of vote? Does it respond to freely constituted associations and organized interests? I think it's legitimate. Or do you just answer, I repeat, to the voters of your party? Here is the difference: once elected - as well as the mayor, the President of the Province when elected by the people, or the President of the Region - you are representatives of everyone. Is it in the general interest? But how does the general interest of the chosen one form? Based on your personal life, your studies, your experiences? The Member of Parliament - and I repeat this in order to remain in the proceedings - must be without a mandate. How is the candidate and then elected Member chosen? From party secretariats? According to a political cursus honorum that the parties take into account? I want to think so. They tell me that in France this happens without any law, so we start as mayor, as president of a superior body, as president of a region, of a department and then we become deputies and senators. I believe that a little political cursus honorum is needed, also because, if the parties took these elements into account, they would choose from people who have accomplished something in life. Once elected, what can you do? Do you know the administrative, economic and social problems of your college and Italy, if you want, why should you mediate with what may be a contingent or particular situation? I also think that there is a difference between being elected in a large city and being elected actually smaller, up to the mountain realities, where there are vast colleges with many kilometers of roads and surface, but completely different problems and objective difficulties to be faced. I wonder: does the elected one have any experience of public administration? Do you know how this works? Do you also have a life experience? That's why I said that forty years to become a senator seem fair to me: because in life one learns perhaps at forty years of age. Even without a degree, are you at least a little self-taught in these subjects? Do you know what you're doing? Does he live political boarding school on Saturdays and Sundays, or does he go by boat? How can you hack it once you're here? PRESIDENT. - The debate is closed. I invite you to conclude, Senator Perosino. PEROSIN (FI-BP). I'm finishing, Mr. President. There is a system for which the Government is necessarily in the hands of the majority and the decree-laws have preferential paths to be converted within sixty days. But the bill often remains in the drawers and when, after one, two or three years, they are lucky enough to be approved, they pass to the other House and while the legislature ends. Sometimes I feel a little swampy, but I believe, despite everything, that those who elected us have put more trust in us than in our parties. And constitutional reform, which is as dry as the one we are going to approve, should create a positive political tension to give us and our citizens some hope. [[Applause from the FI-BP Group]]

ParlaMint-IT_2019-02-06-LEG18-Senato-sed-88.ana.u24	Mr President, ladies and gentlemen of the Senate, in addressing the issue of constitutional reforms, there is a question of whether we are doing the usual rite or whether we are trying to do something authentic this time: if that were the case, in the opinion of Forza Italia and myself, it would certainly be worthy of proper attention. We have a very beautiful Constitution and it could not be otherwise: its genesis and its protagonists have meant that, after the Second World War, Italy had a Constitutional Charter of the highest level and quality, certainly superior to those of many other countries. It is also true, however, that the Constitution is not a totem and Parliament has the right and duty to try to change, especially because times change. It is clear that the country is much worse than it was ten to fifteen years ago and that there is certainly a direct responsibility, in some cases linked to the ineffectiveness of our democracy. Subjective errors have been made and attributable to the political forces, but certainly also system errors, in the functioning of our democracy, which in recent years has proved too slow in reading problems and articulating answers, but also inconsistent in verifying whether the responses it had given to the new social issues were effective and capable of improving the lives of citizens or not. 40% of Italian citizens are not well: you know that, because you won the election for it; you have ridden this situation, stirring up their fears and difficulties. I am thinking of the issues of security, youth unemployment, individual income prospects or families, and the greater inefficiency and ineffectiveness of public services linked to municipalities or health services. In this respect, Parliament and the legislative function have a responsibility and, if you do not give the answers, it means that they did not work. When a constitutional reform is introduced, then, it is done with the aim of improving the situation. Salvador Giner, Brazilian politician, a few years ago, scandalizing the free West, wrote that democracy is not the best form of government ever, but it is certainly the one that guarantees the best compromise between individual freedoms and the security of the citizen. Democratic and liberal Europe is founded on this democratic model, so there is a duty to deepen the issue in Parliament. I ask you and I whether this is just an exercise or a real attempt this time, because the context, ladies and gentlemen, is absolutely different. It is clear that citizens today are at the limit: they have not forgiven and will not forgive a further failure in their ability to articulate answers on major emerging social issues. I am thinking of security, youth unemployment, the degradation of cities and the governance of migratory flows, on which all political forces - including us who have ruled over these twenty years - have a very serious responsibility. In 1992 in Italy there were 52,000 immigrants: today, in 2019, we have 6 million regulars and 700,000 of irregulars. This is an epochal phenomenon, which politics has not been able to govern and on which answers must certainly be given. We are also following the sound rejection of a constitutional referendum, which has shown that citizens do not have a ring in their noses. When I go to school to explain to the fifth year high school kids how Parliament and the legislative process work, I am ashamed. Then I ask myself, and I ask you, whether the problem is the number of Members of Parliament or whether we are legalizing with a perfect, archaic bicameral system, whereby, for example, an amendment can be tabled to the House in the Commission which is rejected and then can be discussed; there is an agenda that is rejected and then can be discussed. There are 24 hours of trust. Basically, months go by to give people a response. I am not talking about legal or illegal things. Deciding on something today or deciding on it in six months is not the same thing, so democracy must become faster. When it comes to constitutional reform and parliamentary institutions, I think - and I really hope that we all agree - that we have a moral obligation and a civil duty to do so in a shared way. ParlaMint-IT_2019-02-06-LEG18-Senato-sed-88.ana.u31	Mr President, members of the Government, ladies and gentlemen, I am speaking in particular to point out to the intelligence of the rapporteur and the proposers of this constitutional reform what I can only consider to be a material error in the drafting of the text. I am referring to including the foreign constituency in the proportional reduction in the number of Members. As many of you know, the foreign constituency is an institutional conquest of Italians abroad and Italians all, which brought six senators to this branch of Parliament and to the Lower House, in Montecitorio, 12 MPs: an extremely small number compared to the total number of Italians living abroad, therefore with a very discriminatory relationship with us. I have a few numbers: compared with 60 million Italians, we currently have 945 MEPs, of whom only 18 are from the foreign constituency. With the effect of this new reform, we would have 600 parliamentarians, but only 12 parliamentarians representing Italians abroad, including 8 in the Chamber of Deputies and 4 in the Senate. With a normal conversion report, these parliamentarians would instead be 52, including 35 in the Chamber of Deputies and 17 in the Senate. I would point out that four senators representing 5.5 million Italians enrolled in the Anagraphe of Italians living abroad (AIRE) represent an aberration, which is further exasperated by the fact that the foreign constituency is divided into 4 divisions. For the Europe distribution, in which 3.3 million Italians registered at AIRE are registered, there would be only one representative in the Senate. I would point out to you that, in international doctrine, there is no criterion, no formula worthy of this name that allows a relationship of this kind. Having only one parliamentary representative in the Senate of the Republic, in a bicameral system, representing 3.3 million Italians in Europe or one senator every 1.4 million citizens, considering the total of the four senators who would represent the 5.5 million Italians enrolled in AIRE, constitutes a democratic aberration. I believe that the Constitutional Court could in no way validate the democratic nature of such a measure. There is a substantial violation of the principle of equality set out in Article 3 of the Constitution, but I also point to a substantial violation of Article 1 of the Constitution, which states that Italy is a democratic Republic. How does a Republic define itself as democratic if it brings a representative of its citizens to Parliament for every 3.3 million people? I would point out that the Italian Constitution does not discriminate against citizens depending on where they are resident: Italian citizens abroad are 100% Italian citizens. I also appeal to the exasperated use of the term "citizens" which makes an important part of this majority: we are citizens and you, who have as your priority the defence of the constitutional, civil and political rights of citizens, are effectively abolishing those of citizens living abroad. [[Applause from Senator Alderisi]] Italy is not made up of 60 million people, it is made up of 60 million people living in Italy and 5.5 million people, officially registered, living abroad. They are also citizens. I would also point out that the figure of 5.5 million Italians residing abroad, recorded by AIRE, has exploded in recent years. In 2006 it was about 3 million and now we have reached 5.5 million. In 2017, according to the official data of AIRE, 250,000 Italian citizens emigrated. There is a lot of talk here about migration, but we always talk about those who come to Italy. Let me tell you that the Italian citizens who go outside Italy are many more, there have been many in history and there are so many in the present. The migratory phenomenon has exploded and is a problem of our days and our territories. There is no Italian territory in which this escape is not present: they emigrate from the North, the Centre and the South, they emigrate young people, men, women, elderly, fifty years old; in short, they all escape. This is not the time to analyze why they run away, since the motivations are many and serious, but it is the time to analyze the fact that, at the moment when this phenomenon has reexploded, this majority cuts the political rights and institutional representation of Italians abroad. It took decades of struggles, which culminated in the Italian Parliament with a victory, the establishment of the foreign constituency, which you are now putting at risk. You are also jeopardising it with a non-action in this area of Parliament, which we continue to denounce repeatedly, but which nothing seems to be able to happen: I am referring to the failure to re-establish the Committee on Italian Affairs abroad. [[Applause from Senator Alderisi]] A Committee which, from five successive legislatures, has the priority of studying, analysing and proposing the issues of Italians abroad and which this political majority, in this legislature, has the responsibility of not having reconstituted yet. Even so, we have reached the point where the Foreign Commission intends to manage the issue of Italians abroad, that is 10% of the Italian population, with an investigation, as are the investigations on the facts of Forteto (with all due respect) and as are other very specific investigations. We want to include 10% of the population in a survey by the Foreign Commission, not by re-establishing the Committee on Italian Affairs abroad. It is a scandal, it is a serious thing; and it is particularly serious that we do not make a different policy, even institutionally, than different things. I repeat that 3,300,000 Italians residing in Europe and 5,500,000 Italians residing abroad are an extremely underestimated figure. In London we are registered at the Consulate General in 380,000, but the English have told us that we are more than 600,000; the same thing happens in Germany, where there are about 850,000 Italians resident, but the Germans turn out to be one and a half million, on the basis of VAT and contracts, i.e. data impossible (here is the whole issue of why we do not subscribe to AIRE). The data are extremely underestimated, so we are definitely talking about more than 10% of the Italian population. These are Italian citizens who cannot be discriminated against further with this reform, compared to what is already in place. I repeat that the constitutionality of this draft reform is, in my opinion, absolutely doubtful, because, by reducing the representation of the foreign constituency, it violates the principle of equality and the principle of democratic intervention. I refer to the principle of proportionality, adequacy and appropriateness between means and ends: there can be no appropriateness between the political representation of the interests of a community of 3,800,000 people with only one representative in Parliament. It's not democratic. I appeal to the intelligence of the rapporteur and those who are pursuing this constitutional reform, which we share in the general principles and have also partly already voted, in the past (the reduction in the number of Members): the foreign constituency, which has exploded and needs urgent attention, cannot be affected in this way. [[Applause from the FI-BP Group.]]

ParlaMint-IT_2019-02-06-LEG18-Senato-sed-88.ana.u33	Mr President, ladies and gentlemen, the draft law that we are called upon to debate comes in an area which only apparently concerns one aspect of detail of our constitutional system. The reduction in the number of Members and senators directly invests in the quality of our parliamentary democracy in terms of the representativeness of the Chambers and their full functionality. In the Constitution - Mortati recalled from the introductory report in the second Sub-Committee of the constituent assembly - everything is held: the form of State with the form of Government, the protection of rights with the guarantee of the fulfilment of duties, freedom with equality and justice. I want to repeat it to you: freedom with equality and justice. Every technical detail is part of a broader plan, which frames our coexistence and the organization of our democracy within very precise values and principles, which are equal social dignity, the protection of minorities, the guarantee in the face of the excesses of the majority. I want to repeat this: the guarantee in front of the majority's accesses. [[Applause from the PD Group]] Therefore, even a seemingly marginal intervention, if not adequately included in a broader awareness of constitutional principles and values, risks having a major impact on the preservation of our fundamental Charter. I remember well, I have been in this House for five years, the cries of colleagues and colleagues who are sitting today in the benches of the government and the majority, ready to denounce the risks of democratic breakup, even of slipping towards dictatorship, in relation to our proposal for reform of Part II of the Constitution. An ambitious reform, of course, with its limits, of course, but undoubtedly technically valid and above all fully integrated into a broader plan, aimed at ensuring democracy, representativeness and efficiency of the constitutional system. It was not the love of the Constitution that moved you, but only your miserable electoral gain, and that is why, in a hurry, you incardinate this terrible text. [[Applause from the PD Group]] You will recall, ladies and gentlemen, that nothing was written by chance in the Constitution, not even the numbers: the fixing of the number of deputies in 630 and of senators in 315 was the result of careful and meditated work in the Constitutional Assembly, which ended with the adoption of the constitutional law in 1963. The second sub-committee and the Assembly, then, long wondered about the most appropriate criteria for identifying the appropriate number of Members and senators, setting out a path that, even today, must inspire us. The mothers and the constituent fathers were not guided by financial concerns, nor by the need to pursue consent, although even at the time they did not lack in the country and in the current palaces of power strong anti-politics and anti-parliamentaryism. The only concern that guided our constituents was to provide the country with a Parliament that can function effectively, ensuring maximum representativeness and maximum efficiency. Those chosen were not random numbers, but based on the findings of the last census available at the time, that of 1936. They are not numbers thrown there at random, but structural elements of a democratic political culture, a sign of the deep care that those who preceded us wanted to put in the construction of the institutional architecture of the Republic. The search for adequate proportionality to the population is an instrument that ensures the representativeness of the Chambers, their effective responsibility - responsibility for all of us, elected and elected - and proximity to the electricians and voters: in times of crisis of legitimacy of politics and representative institutions, we should not forget these fundamental teachings. What happens, however, with the reform that the majority are proposing to us today? Members would be reduced to 400 and senators to 200: a far greater reduction than all those proposed by the various proposals for revision that have followed over the years and that stood at around 500 Members and 250 Senators. Our proposal for reform in the last parliamentary term also reduced the number of senators to 95, but it did so in the framework of a comprehensive reform of bicameralism and above all, precisely because of the reduction in the number of senators, it did not affect the number of Members, who remained fixed at 630. With the reduction to 400 Members and 200 Senators, the proportionality ratio is exaggerated: according to Eurostat data, we will end up with one Member per 151,000 inhabitants and one Senator per 302,000 inhabitants. I am ready to listen when you tell us that all this does not matter, in the time of the Network and, on the other hand, you are the same people who, not later than a few weeks ago, declared the objective of overcoming Parliament itself within a few years. That is why, and it is to this end, your concern must be read about the proposed referendum of popular initiative, the real way to break down representative democracy. Here, everything is held, as the Mothers and the constituent Fathers knew. Your plan to dismantle parliamentary democracy in this country is linked to your unconfessed desire to repeal the mandatory mandate ban, to your indecent parliamentary practice. [[Applause from the PD Group]] Just remember, as Mr Errani did earlier, what you have combined on the budget law and recently on the simplification decree. You want a parliament of puppets, remote-controlled puppets. We cannot ignore this when we reflect on a proposal for a constitutional revision which, on the surface, may seem reasonable, but it is not. [[Applause from the PD Group. ParlaMint-IT_2019-02-06-LEG18-Senato-sed-88.ana.u39	Mr President, I must confess a certain dismay in the face of this debate. I come from the last parliamentary term, during which we proposed a comprehensive and articulated reform of the Constitution, which was approved by Parliament and then rejected, in the referendum, by the people. We have experienced that reform as a serious attempt to modernise our democracy, but with the clear will to reverse the values contained in our Constitution, making them real and as current as possible and implemented in the concrete life of citizens. Coming from that experience and finding myself now in front of a completely different scenario - I must say upset - I try to understand, in this disarray, what are the elements and contributions that I can and can lead to this debate. You see, as we examine some of the changes that seem untidy - the House of Deputies is discussing the proposed referendum, while here we are considering the measure to reduce the number of parliamentarians - other things happen that are going to change the fundamental relations within our State. Only a few days ago, during the examination of the so-called simplification decree, we approved an amendment that, in the matter of energy, made the regions of the North exactly like the Regions with special statute. The management, in fact, of the hydroelectric power stations that until yesterday had only the Regions under special statute has been extended also to the other Regions. Have we changed the Constitution? Have we discussed differentiated regionalism? Did we go into the form of the state? Did we say any of this? No: we have made an amendment and the power stations in Veneto are now managed exactly like those in Bolzano. All this happens with good peace of the Southern Legists, who see in rage of people passing resources centrally managed directly to the North Regions. The League is making proselytes. I see a natural and, perhaps, just and justified push. Mr Calderoli was mentioned earlier, and I wanted to do so when he first approached. The drive to simplify and rebel against the complexity of today's world is natural. At the time there were no social media and, therefore, it was necessary to set fire to cubic meters of paper and to say that we removed tens of thousands of useless laws that made us and make life difficult. We, however, are going in a direction where the world becomes complex, increasingly complex and compared to this complexity we are not putting forward solutions that try to deal with it in the right way. We are trying to rebel against this complexity by putting in place simplifications that are not solutions. This is the subject we are dealing with today, in my opinion. It is true that there are problems that we have to face today. Last legislature, with our reforms, we were still in the wake of a reflection that has been made in our country for decades to try to modernise democracy. Today, after the elections, we are in another world where, compared to the evolution of our democracy on the basis of the content, concepts and values of our Constitution, we propose a path of simplification that cannot lead to solutions. This is the theme. Let us address these problems with a thorough reflection. The issue of the crisis of democracy has already been addressed in the debate. It is true and we want to face it. I think it's a good theme, and I agree with Senator Errani. We need to understand how to deal with this debate. I wonder where the constitutionalists are. Are you under the tables? [[Applause from the PD Group and Senator Conzatti]] You put yourself under the tables because the procedure, when an earthquake comes, is hiding under the table right away? Is an earthquake coming into our democracy and hiding under the tables? No, I think we need to put some thinking into it because the answer to the simplification push is the de-responsibility of the political class. You don't want to take the responsibility to decide anymore. You say that you will do things, but then the people, whom you will ask and express themselves, will decide. In a complex world we are seeing in England the people who decide. And it wasn't even a complicated question: "inside or out." But behind it or beyond, there was the complexity of the world, of political and economic relations, of geopolitics and of decades of history made in Europe between states, cultures, religions and peoples. And the result is what we know and still doesn't find a solution. Let us then face the complexity that the world proposes to us, but let us make sure that the institutions are able to cope with it with new instruments, by inverting the concepts and values of our Constitution and giving our children the opportunity to have a better future. [[Applause from the PD Group and Senator Conzatti.]]
