ParlaMint-IT_2019-02-06-LEG18-Senato-sed-88.ana.u43	Mr President, ladies and gentlemen, I think it is very important that we give a thorough thought to the impact of the constitutional change that this House is debating. The one proposed by the majority is a merely seemingly limited intervention, presented as a simplification or a reduction in policy costs, but that is not the case. Your reform, colleagues in the majority, has nothing to do with the overcoming of the bicameral system; nothing to do with the constitutional reform of the centre-left - as has already been widely said yesterday and today - which was intended instead to overcome a mechanism that has become cumbersome, in order instead to introduce a more efficient system. The way in which it is proposed to follow the majority reform affects a number of important constitutional values and is such that the squeeze of parliamentary representation is excessive. Political pluralism is the essence of democracy, as it expresses the different orientations of the electoral body. The difficult balance between the different needs requires attention, not least because fragmentation, which reduces governance, certainly does not depend on the institutions, while the reduction of about one third of the members of Parliament - as proposed - has very strong repercussions. The current composition of Parliament does not hinder the possibility of developing the instruments of direct democracy already present in the constitutional order, while intervention of this magnitude risks impoverishing the democratic system and, in particular, making some voices such as those of indigenous national minorities recognized and protected by the Constitution and the Statutes of autonomy disappear. For this reason, ladies and gentlemen, I have to express my deep concern on the basis of something that deserves at least to be corrected. I would like to focus on the political representation of the indigenous Slovenian-speaking national minority which insists in the Region under the special statute of Friuli-Venezia Giulia. The constituent legislator wisely considered to protect this reality. You want to crush minorities: you started with the press and now you want to take away our right to stand. The recognition of special autonomy on the one hand and the inclusion of this principle among the fundamental principles of the Republic are expressed in Article 6, according to which the Republic protects linguistic minorities with appropriate rules. It is a programmatic commitment that has led to the adoption of positive protection measures in the special statutes and in the laws implementing the Constitution, such as Law No. 38 of 2001, on the norms to protect the Slovenian linguistic minority of the Region Friuli-Venezia Giulia which, in Article 26, expressly commits the legislator providing that: The electoral laws for the election of the Senate of the Republic and of the Chamber of Deputies lay down rules to facilitate access to the representation of candidates belonging to the Slovenian minority. The approval of the constitutional changes, with the reduction of one third of the number of parliamentarians, is such as to compress the forms of protection of linguistic minorities recognized in the Regions with special autonomy, according to Articles 2, 3 and 6 of the Constitution, and for the Slovenian minority by Article 3 of Constitutional Law No 1 of 31 January 1963 of adoption of the Special Statute of the Friuli-Venezia Giulia Region. With the proposed modification - according to the projections reported by the Senate Studies Service in the dossier dedicated to the proposals in question - Friuli-Venezia Giulia would have, in proportion to the population resident in the Chamber of Deputies, a reduction of 38.5%, going from the 13 seats currently held to 8 seats. The Senate would be reduced by 28.6 percent, from the current 7 to 5 seats. In this way, taking into account the territorial context, the basic requirement would be to allow the Slovenian linguistic and national minority to participate freely and autonomously in electoral competitions and to contribute, in accordance with the constitutional principles - as translated in a positive form by the aforementioned Article 26 of Law 38 of 2001 - to elect a representative in Parliament that can bring into the democratic debate the need of the indigenous community to maintain and enhance social relations, life and minority culture, expression of cultural diversity, integral part of the nation, also the Italian one - of the country, therefore - and universal heritage to be preserved. Ladies and gentlemen, I repeat that, as it stands, the text risks creating an unbalanced situation with regard to the fundamental principles of the Republic contained in the first 12 articles of the Constitution, on which the other rules of law are based: the principle of equality, enshrined in Article 3, and the pluralist principle, enshrined in Article 2, combined with Article 6, which - as I have just mentioned - is committed in particular to protecting linguistic minorities. The opportunity to introduce corrective measures to ensure that there is a guarantee of a destination in the territories where the minority - in my Slovenian case - traditionally insists on an elected senator responds to a principle of substantial equality that does not affect the principle of equality of voting, but makes it effective, taking into account inter alia the internationalistic framework that concerned the territory in question, in particular the London Memorandum in 1954 between Italy, Great Britain and the United States, and the 1975 Osimo Treaty, whose ratification was authorised by Law No 73 of 1977; provisions that would also be consistent with the international instruments and principles of the Council of Europe on the right to vote of ethnic minorities. I therefore hope that the debate will offer scope for these aspects, allowing a serene evaluation of the proposals I have made, partly because the speed of the debate in the Commission has not allowed the necessary insights into these issues. I believe that this House has the opportunity to review the matter and to consider the impact of a much more incisive constitutional reform than it appears for the important repercussions on the constitutional order, also in the light of the international constraints that protect the indigenous minorities, allowing them to pay attention to the cultural value they express. Ladies and gentlemen, the conditions must be introduced to allow the Slovenian minority to participate freely and autonomously in electoral competitions and to contribute, in accordance with constitutional principles, as translated in a positive form by the aforementioned Article 26 of Law No 38 of 2001, and in coherence with the obligations assumed by our country at international level, to elect a representative by reviewing the text in question. [[Applause from the PD Group. Congratulations.]]

ParlaMint-IT_2019-02-06-LEG18-Senato-sed-88.ana.u68	Mr President, I have to say that speaking to a semi-empty Chamber is never a good feeling, but we still try. Today we enter into the merit of a law - the classic flag law of a perennial electoral campaign that is continuing - and of a rule that has been flagrant for a long time, which can only be the linear cut of parliamentarians, senators and parliamentarians: a cut that does not come into the substance - we have heard so many speeches this morning and yesterday - of a vision and a reformer project of the whole constitutional system, but is limited, as proposed in the text that we have read, to a simple abolition of parliamentarians. A true reformism should enter into the merit of perfect bicameralism, just as we did with our reform of the center-left: a reformism that has been sought for many years, but unfortunately has never come to completion. Here there is no trace of a more complex and comprehensive reform; we are talking only of reaching 200 senators and 400 Members. Nor is there any trace of the diversification of the roles of the two chambers - as we had proposed - by providing for the House of Deputies to legislate and the House of Senators instead to listen to the territories, because they had to be heard differently. There's a mere cut here. There is no idea of speeding up, as another colleague has already said. Do you think speeding up means cutting off senators and MPs? This is not a system. You certainly need to review the system, but not just by cutting it. Do you want to signal populism? That's the most important thing you've ever done. It is an answer to the anger against the caste, as you have always said. Unfortunately around there is a very different design, a design that sees behind a progressive departure from politics. The progressive removal and dismantling of representative democracy is a plan that is now being pursued by this Government in particular, with the idea that parliamentarians have become a more, a harp for those who want to decide. We have seen it well with the budget law, when there was no opportunity to discuss it. A very large amendment has been tabled, on which we have not even been able to incorporate further amendments, because we have been prevented from doing so. The same possibility has also been denied to the majority Members who, although they have prerogatives, have given up on it, leaving the Commission to work undisturbedly. Thanks to the intervention we made as a Democratic Party there was an important reminder, followed by a very important intervention in the decree simplifications by the President of the Senate: if we had not had this initiative, no one could have said anything and would have gone on without any kind of intervention. The measure under consideration does not change the bicameral system or the perfect bicameralism - as we have said - by distinguishing it, to give life also to the voice of the territories. No, none of this, and even more of the self-governments - we heard the speaker earlier - who have a right of voice. Instead, the most important representations are cut, as are the high representations of our citizens abroad, with an important measure that sees them move away from their state, from their nation. A more organic form therefore there is no, it does not exist. Unfortunately - I must say - even from this point of view it is a drawing that comes from afar. I remember cuts to a participatory democracy that came even from 2010 (there was still the Government with the League): at that time - I remember it well, because at that time I was an administrator - they even cut the districts, with a participatory democracy and close to the citizens. You see, so, how long has this drawing been coming? I hope that the society outside will wake up, even if it is not interested at this time in the important speech we are making, because we are intervening on the Constitution. Unfortunately, what matters to people outside is just giving up against Members, giving up, because we are simply seen as a cost of politics. The society at this moment is silent, poisoned by the hatred perpetrated against everyone: we are at a time when everyone is against everyone and nobody cares about what we are doing, let alone the parliamentarians, who have always been considered a caste: moreover, those who are now part of the government have always considered the parliamentarians a caste, giving them up. Now you're in the government. Are you happy when you hear yourself calling yourself lame? I have never been happy to be called idle, not least because those who know well the work of the parliamentarians know that it is not a dull job at all: we start the morning with the Commissions, we arrive here in the Chamber, we try to be always present and to be on the territory. Those who do this work with satisfaction and a sense of service towards their citizens know it and do not believe it is a useless job, as you have always thought it was and how you are making people think about it outside of here: this is the big problem. There is no need, therefore, to call oneself or to speak out as a spokesperson for the citizens: we have always been. This is representative democracy. This means being elected and, as you have been elected by the people, we too have been elected. I have my own area of reference, my citizens waiting for me to ask a question and for that question to be answered, although unfortunately this is happening with ever longer times and enormous delays. Know that there was no need, because before you were there we had already done things too. Unfortunately, they have been rejected; the people have rightly decided. But know that we too have done a reform and we wanted to do it altogether, really putting the "i" on the mark. I am also referring to the fact that I have thought of a more national and less fragmented health care; a health care that could have been listening to the whole of the national territory, and not in a heterogeneous way as is now done in full autonomy by the Regions. So we are not seeing our Parliament being deported. And we are not seeing that, only by consensus, this Parliament is being emptied of what is, on the other hand, its most important prerogative, namely to bring the demands of our citizens to this House. Democracy has been paid dearly, and history teaches us that. The constituent Fathers had not by chance made such a complex plant, so that there would be a management of the State and the nation that could support and support itself. There is no taboo saying that we have to cut the parliamentarians. But cut like this, in a linear way, like when you cut expenses in a linear way without intervening, it doesn't make sense, it's sterile. It really is always a matter of electoral consensus. I hope that this time will end - it will have to end - because it is not possible to manage a state in this way, i.e. only with electoral ads like those we have seen these days, opening up as if there were a magical bulletin board, or pulling out a card. Know, among other things, that it had already been done by previous governments, and it was not done for the first time. I must say that at this point we are not simply reducing the number of Members. We have tabled amendments which intervene in a much more complex form, for a reform that really would create a faster plant. But on a precious asset, what democracy is, we are not with you on this path. We have too great a value, and the value of democracy is too precious and must be preserved for the future of our children as well. So, either you listen to us or we're not. And on the concept of the renewal of the system it seems important to me to say some things, even if politics is under fire today, precisely because the renewal of the Italian political system has failed. Of course, the reduction in the number of Members cannot but be discussed well, but within the framework of a system revolution. No political force has spoken of this in the last 40 years. On the other hand, we were the first to complete, thanks to the tenacity of our President Berlusconi, a true and serious reform that reviewed and unified our political-institutional system. Italy had and needs a reduction in the number of parliamentarians, bringing their numbers back to the level of other democracies. Above all, there was the need for a more sober policy, particularly in behaviour, which every day we see being exaggerated by Members of Parliament and the Government. [[Applause from the FI-BP Group]] Above all, the data tell us that it was necessary and necessary to avoid that the excessive number of Members of Parliament, together with the age-old question of bicameralism, continues to make the process of approving the draft laws cumbersome, complex and extremely fragmented. We, as parliamentarians, every day living in parliamentary activity, however, know perfectly well that, when a law has a rapid process, it is because the Government proposes it is in agreement with all the passages. Therefore, despite the debate in the Commission, the tabling of amendments and other passages, it is well known that, despite bicameralism, a measure can be adopted within 30 or 60 days at most. It is when things have happened in this legislature too, and before that, that the process of examining the measures goes on two or two and a half years, because of a constant rebound, because there is no single voice in the Government. With regard to the reduction of the fantastic costs of politics, I hope that not only the lowest cost of reducing the number of Members of Parliament has been assessed, but also the possible higher burdens that could be incurred as a result of the changes that the legislative machinery will have to face. On the other hand, it is easy to believe that the savings resulting from the reduction of the so-called political class (the caste) are automatic. Has anyone, however, verified what has been spared in the past as a result of the reforms that have led to the reduction of the members of the various representative collegial bodies, both regional and local? Maybe not. Furthermore, was it not necessary to imagine that, if a reduced legislative body could acquire greater authority, the choice of citizens' representatives should be made with greater selection capacity? However, we know that this is certainly not the intention of the Rousseau platform. I imagine that we all want a more authoritative Parliament and a better-chosen political class, especially in these times of rampant ignorance. But, beyond the doubts raised in the Commission during the hearings, I would like to say with the same sincerity to those who think, here and outside, that just a tweet - practically this is being talked about and not a reform - is enough to strengthen the political institutions and the feeling of our people and the people, who are out of reality. It is also out of reality in demonstrating that it cannot speak and listen to the thousands of people we have the honor, the venture, the pleasure and the duty to meet every day. We need more, ladies and gentlemen. We need more politicians - and fewer showmen and millantators - than we know how to motivate a people that is largely distrusted, disoriented and indefatigable. We need new, courageous and revolutionary criteria, channels and methods of democratic representation and pluralism in a complex nature and in an evolved nation like ours. And when I talk about new criteria for democratic representation, I am certainly not talking about online voting. In this respect, I believe that, rather than talking about referendums, it would have been better to discuss the direct election of the heads of state - I am referring to the Head of State or the Head of Government - which are courageous and not propaganda choices. This is what would be useful and what the whole centre-right had proposed in its programme; decisions that can break the rules to make citizens - yes - decide who is called to play top roles and who has been good and who is not. Forza Italia has always been in favour of giving the floor to citizens, when citizens are really motivated to ask for it. Too bad that the other branch of Parliament is discussing a constitutional reform that does not respond at all to the announced spirit. I fear rather that this is a prelude to propaganda at the Casaleggio, where it is hoped that, one day, referendums will only be made via the Net, without quorum, even if the quorum - thank God - has been introduced, even if not in the forms that we - for the truth - would have wanted and should have been. We do not want the Network referendums and we continue to prefer ordinary people, possibly prepared, to the trolls. The illusion of the creation of a distorted democracy with direct participation has led to the establishment of an instrument that would create a perfectly contrary effect to the principles that have been put in place by our constituent Fathers. This is the difference between a piece of paper full of clichés and useless goodness and a Constitutional Charter. The Constitution can be written or rewritten in whole or in part, but only those who have great ideas or, possibly, ideas - perhaps that too - can do so. Who has great ideas meets with the great opposing ideas of those who think differently - this is democracy - and not with those who are devoid of thought or bearer of weak thought. Colleagues, without great ideas and a great and uncontrollable love for our homeland I have the feeling that we are talking about nothing or, of course, very little, perhaps at the level of some propaganda tweets with written "made" and - I would add, however - hurt. ParlaMint-IT_2019-02-06-LEG18-Senato-sed-88.ana.u77	She is registered to speak Senator Gallone, whom I thank for coming promptly. You have the right to do so.
ParlaMint-IT_2019-02-06-LEG18-Senato-sed-88.ana.u78	Thank you, Mr President, as long as we still have the power to speak, it is right to be there. Mr President, representative of the Government, ladies and gentlemen, Parliament is not a club service to be applied for: Parliament is the most sacred place of a rule of law. It is the place where the laws are born, the place where it affects the lives of citizens, the place of democratic representation. Having said that, we are here today to deal for the umpteenth time - the eighth, to be precise, starting with the Bozzi Commission of the so-called first Republic - with the same issue: the reduction in the number of parliamentarians. A choice that we are generally in favour of since it was made, as has already been said many times during the debate and with almost identical points of view, also by the Berlusconi Government in 2005, although in a very different way. The reduction in the number of Members and senators is therefore a subject that has been addressed many times in this House, with choices which are similar to the number each time (in this case, respectively, from 630 to 400 and from 315 to 200). But the numbers are not the problem or the issue on which there is to discuss, as long as we can still discuss, as I said before. This is a delicate decision in any case, because it involves the basic criteria on which our democratic system is based: the parliamentary representativeness and the balance of the powers of our rule of law, criteria which cannot be overcome by direct representation experiments, such as the elimination of the quorum referendary or anything else. We are in favour of a reduction in the number of Members of Parliament, but within the framework of a broader, more organic, overall, rational and constitutional reform of the state system. In fact, to limit itself to reducing the number of Members of Parliament tout court, will simply mean to unbalance a fundamental balance in order to guarantee democracy and, above all, popular sovereignty and will simply mean to create macro-colleges which, by force of things, will create dispersity and distance between the elector and elected, thus to the detriment of popular sovereignty and representation. In fact, if it is true, as my colleague Mr Biasotti also mentioned in a speech that preceded me that our country has one of the most numerous parliaments in Europe, it is equally true that it is placed in the twentieth place by criteria of representativeness, precisely for the relationship between elected and voters; therefore, the reduction of parliamentarians should be accompanied at the same time by a series of fundamental actions of organic and balanced modification of the state system as a whole. First, the strengthening of local autonomy, mortified by PD-led governments, first of all the Renzi Government, restoring, first of all to the Provinces, the role of subsidiary intermediate body, mortified by the blows of laws that little citizenship finds in our constitutional system. The second is the strengthening of the regional autonomy, endowed - I remember - with legislative autonomy, which recalls all the local realities to the responsibility of the government of their territories, without it being possible this unworthy dischargebarile between South and North and vice versa. The third and most delicate point is a revisiting of the role of the judiciary, especially in the civil field, with an increased role of the supreme court, through legislation that provides a binding nomophilachia for the lower grades close to the common law, that allows to find, to those who want to invest in our country, a legal and regulatory framework of reasonable certainty. In addition, fundamental to maintaining the balance of the rule of law is the fact that parliamentary relief must be matched by a lightening of the size and pervasiveness of the Executive and its structures, also providing for a rigid correlation between the number of Ministers, Vice-Ministers, Undersecretaries and the number of parliamentarians and a consequent reduction of the central bureaucratic apparatuses that stifle citizens. All this without renouncing the unmistakable role of representation that is guaranteed to Parliament. I repeat that we are in favour of reducing the number of parliamentarians, because when the constituent legislator defined the composition of Parliament he had a different country, in which the Regions were entities on paper and other problems had to find citizenship in the Constitutional Charter: think of Austrian pressures for the protection of ethnic minorities in South Tyrol. Today, with a changed and changing state in which the Regions, in homage to the principle of subsidiarity, operate with legislative autonomy, it is possible to think of a reduction of the legislative weight of the Parliament and the central government. Ça va sans say that such a reform cannot and must not take place for the sake of economic savings, even if important, because democracy is too important a tool to be subjected to purely mercantile choices; in the same way it cannot be a functional choice to an increase in the power of the Executive that goes to the detriment of the role that the representatives of the people called to represent it in this in the other House are called to play. A government of the Executive has the bad taste of the dictatorship, whether it be monocratic or that kind of diarchy with a social mediator that we are living in: a government in which the two leaders of the parties decide everything and proceed by decree-laws and trusts, imposing on the country their will, making succube and non-existent the role of Parliament. Remember that taking away dignity and role in Parliament means taking away dignity and voice from the people. I think of that freedom that Lincoln spoke of in the famous Gettysburg speech: May this nation (...) have a rebirth of freedom and that the idea of a People's Government, from the people, for the people should not perish from the earth. Freedom passes through a democratic government: of people through universal suffrage, from the people through elective representation and for the people and therefore free from choices guided by propagandistic logics aimed at the self-preservation of power. In this context of renewed or restored freedom and autonomy of society from a central state, in too many nineteenth-century ways, it is not only opportune but even fair to think of a reduction of Parliament in numerical terms and an overall reform of the state system. There are many reasons why direct democracy cannot operate rebus sic statibus and for which it is absurd to think of the referendum without quorum. The first and most important is that a referendum without quorum deeply and irreparablely undermines the majority democratic principle. If the majority principle, i.e. the reason why the minority should be subject to the will of the majority, has already been a concern for lawyers (Ruffini for all), the transition to a minority model in which the majority must comply with the will of a minority, whatever it may be, even appears perverse. To participate or not in a referendum is not an act of disinterest with regard to a topic, but a manifestation of freedom with respect to a subject promoted by a minority, to which I cannot, as a citizen, be called to express an opinion forcefully, even if I think that it is absurd or of no interest. In order to make a reduction to absurd, so useful when it comes to simplifying, if any minority were to promote a referendum to make yellow shoes mandatory for all citizens, it would be absurd to expect me to vote in running, otherwise I would run the risk of having to adapt to the choices of the minority that promoted the referendum. To this reason an even more important one is added, that of informational asymmetry. The reason why the institution of parliamentary delegation exists or in local authorities is due to the fact that the elector entrusts his/her electorate with the task of carrying out diligently, and bearing in mind the reasons of the person who elected him/her, the necessary insights on a subject, while he/she continues to do his/her work and therefore does not have the time or the desire to do so continuously. What the voter expects from his representatives is precisely that they, dedicating their time and their civil passion to it, represent him in the choices. It is an indisputable fact that opens up the issue of the quality of representation, an ancient theme such as Greek philosophy, but that cannot be tackled, unfortunately, if we want - as we want - to remain in the democratic sphere. If a citizen feels, freely and in full consciousness, that he is represented by an unprepared person, or that he has never given anything to society before, he is free to do so knowing that the consequences of the choices that will be made by those who represent him will be first and foremost his own. ParlaMint-IT_2019-02-06-LEG18-Senato-sed-88.ana.u87	Mr President, I am speaking in this debate on constitutional reform with the fear and reverence that those who have the awareness that the Constitution defines the rules of our civil and democratic coexistence by establishing our civil liturgy, where the word "liturgy" in ancient Greece meant action for the people. We are discussing the proposal to reduce the number of Members. It is a proposal that has been put forward by several parties over the last thirty years, which, in principle, I am not opposed to. It is a proposal, however, which has always been part of a broader analysis of the need for reform of our fundamental Charter. If we look at the history of the attempts to reform our Constitution (from the Commission Bozzi of 1983 to the Commission De Mita-Iotti of 1993, from the Committee Speroni in 1994 to the Commission D'Alema in 1997, from the reform Berlusconi in 2005 to the Commission of Wise Men in 2013, to the bill Renzi-Boschi in 2014), all the attempts put in place were developed with an overall view of the constitutional structure. Regardless of whether or not the political aspects were shared, they were solid and clear formulations. All included changes to the functioning of Parliament, as well as the number of Members. These proposals have been rejected because of contingent facts, such as an early end to the legislature, political disagreements or rejections of popular referendums have taken place. What is the vision behind this proposal? The reduction of the costs of the policy, by the same admission of the proposers. It cannot be said that this is a vision, because we cannot see how our democracy can work better, by limiting ourselves to a reduction in the number of Members. It is enough to see him at this time in this House, and we are a few hundred senators who are talking about an important issue. Does it seem to you that this small number of presences allow us to dialogue better and seriously discuss the constitutional functioning of our democracy? I don't think so. I said that the reduction in the costs of politics is behind this reform proposal, but the cost of the operation of Parliament is not related to the costs of politics, but to those of democracy: if this difference is not clear and is distorted and hidden from the citizens, what is it aiming at? The costs of politics - like those you are supporting, after having filled Palazzo Chigi with a large number of collaborators dedicated to communication, never seen before - will not decrease due to the possible approval of this constitutional reform. So what's the goal? The costs of politics that are incurred by foraging a private company, which gives political input and suggests to some Members, with a daily prontuary, what to say and what to do, will not diminish as a result of the possible approval of this constitutional reform. The costs of politics are different from those of democracy, this is really demagogy. If we relate this proposal for constitutional reform to the one under discussion in the House of Members on referendums to amend the quorum for repealing the quorum, we cannot but wonder how we can achieve the objective of reducing costs - according to your will - as the inevitable increase in referendum proposals will lead to a logical increase in costs for citizens to express themselves. And beware: if you think that it is enough to have citizens express electronically to claim that the costs will decrease, you are mistaken; net of the investment costs to have the necessary tools to express themselves electronically, the bulk of the cost of any democratic consultation would not be given by the paper to print the ballots, which would be spared, but by the staff involved. In fact, as experienced in Lombardy, with the first electronic vote in Italy concerning the consultative referendum for autonomy, one cannot avoid to bear the costs for the staff who manage the seat (president, surveyors, secretaries), the Forces of the order called to ensure that the voting operations take place in the seats in a correct manner and without accidents of any kind or the hours of overtime to be reimbursed to the municipalities for the work carried out by the staff in charge of the electoral offices. In Lombardy, a Legist guide, who boasts to be the most efficient Region of Italy, the electronic vote for the referendum on autonomy, net of the investment of 23 million euros for the purchase of 24,000 tablets necessary for the vote, has cost 24 million euros: a considerable amount, if we take into account that the Lombardy Region itself spends 50 million euros in the three years 2017-2019 to finance the rates of public transport. If we measure the costs of the efficient Lombardy to the entire country, we draw from it that an electronic referendum - always net of the investment costs for the necessary machinery and software - would cost the country about 140 million euros for each consultation, which would multiply due to the reform being discussed in the House. But what savings in the costs of politics or democracy are you talking about, then? What you propose is pure demagogy, the word of which the dictionary Treccani gives the following definition: The political practice tending to obtain the consent of the masses by flattering their aspirations, especially economic ones, with promises that are difficult to achieve. This is a method, concludes the Treccani, which represents the corrupted form of democracy or a simulation of this deed. A simulation: here's the goal, simulating democracy. In my public experience I come from a significant administrative commitment as mayor of my city - Cernusco sul Naviglio - and as vice mayor of the metropolitan city of Milan, a concrete experience that allows to measure the effects and consequences of the norms. When, in 1993, the reform introduced into our system the direct election of the mayor and the president of the Province, that important reform, perhaps the best success on the institutional structures of our country, also involved a substantial revision of the number of members of the municipal councils and of the functions assigned to council, junta and mayor. It was therefore an organic reform and if, as said, the reform works is due to this organicity. There was no intervention to reduce the members of the municipal councils for a financial, cash issue. Moreover, let me say that what is still recognized today as an allowance of office to the mayors with respect to the responsibilities that each of them bears every day, in the face of its own citizens and in the face of the law, does not compare with what is perceived by other institutional levels, it does not really compare, but we must have the courage to recognize these differences and these responsibilities and to value them properly, not to keep them for convenience because they are not heard by the masses. This is also demagogy. I shall end by addressing the Presidency and the Government to consider the amendments that the Democratic Party has tabled, which are intended to accompany the reduction in the number of parliamentarians with a change in other aspects, in a specific way of undifferentiated bicameralism. I would like to recall the words of Dossetti, one of the constituent Fathers, who had participated in the Resistance and who, speaking in 1988 of the Charter that he had helped to write, argued: We have done the Constitution in a spirit of renewal, of modernity, at least in some points, at least in some parts. (...) Certain structures such as absolute bicameralism, such as the referendum, such as the configuration of the Constitutional Court were undoubtedly structures of which one could already, to some extent, see then that they would have slowed down much and weakened much the decision-making possibilities that even modern times impose on those who have political responsibility. These, spoken by a constituent Father, are the nodes on which to intervene, not the number of parliamentarians. Therefore, for the sake of our democracy and of concrete and effective action, I would ask the Presidency and the Government to proceed also by accepting the amendments which, by broadening the debate, do not only consider the reduction in the number of Members, but take overall the aspects that allow our democracy to function better and the constitutional arrangements. [[Applause from the PD Group]]

ParlaMint-IT_2019-02-06-LEG18-Senato-sed-88.ana.u94	Senator Pichetto Fratin is enlisted to speak. You have the right to do so.
ParlaMint-IT_2019-02-06-LEG18-Senato-sed-88.ana.u95	Mr President, ladies and gentlemen, why this measure? Why do we want to reduce the number of Members of Parliament? The reports shall state the various reasons for this. Basically, we are talking about increasing efficiency, increasing productivity, rationalising public expenditure and making our Parliament less baffled. In the same way, one wonders why the constituent Fathers, in 1948, predicted this structure, this institutional architecture, dictating a Constitution that gave freedom to citizens, after the terrible period of the World War, but also having the balance, at that time, to take into account what was good in the previous period, which could be considered defeated and compared to which it was necessary to change. I recall, in this sense, the municipal and provincial system, the forecast of the Regions, the French-style organization of our institutions, with prefectual control (in fact the Napoleonic organization), but also the decision to maintain fundamental norms of civil coexistence. For example, I refer to the civil code and invite you to read the text of 1 January 1942, written in the middle of the war, which was a masterpiece, for its balance and for its smoothness and readable by all. Of course, in 1946 an article on morganatic marriage was repealed, because there was no longer the king to marry and therefore there was no longer the problem of who could become queen. Do we have to change? Should our system be made more modern and adapted to the times? Yes, we are in favour of change and we were already, like Forza Italia, at the end of the 1990s, at the time when a great debate began, together with colleagues of the League, on the federal model of State. However, most of us voted for the 2001 constitutional reform: my party was not in favour, but I voted in favour, because it was a step forward in a change, an adaptation and a modernisation of our Constitution. But always, when these reforms have come to fruition, we have been attentive to the burdens and the counterweights, because counterweights, in a democracy and an institutional organization, are used to avoid exaggerations. This model of balance, weights and counterweights in our Constitution, is what allowed Italy to become the seventh or eighth economy of the world, starting from a condition of rags, as we were in 1945. It has also allowed us to become champions of civil rights: I would like to remind you that it is Parliament that has voted on the law on divorce or abortion. They were confirmed by the people in the referendum, but it was Parliament that discussed them and voted on them. As a country we have also become champions of freedom, a country where the social elevator also worked. Excuse me for digression: I too am a lack of workers, two arms stolen from agriculture - then it was said in a denigratory sense, while today this definition qualified a little'more - and I was able to afford to study and to graduate. This was because a balance had been created in the country, a mechanism of social growth had been created, a mechanism of challenge, in which every family and every individual could try. Do we have to touch this balance? Today we are talking about the part of the legislative power: should it be changed? Sure. The same reform of the Rules of Procedure of the Senate, done in the past legislature, has intervened. Should we strengthen the Commission? Okay. The speed of change in our society also determines the need to have, probably, a stronger, more immediate and more active Executive. Should we have the courage to assess the independence of the judiciary, not by contextualizing it, but by a multi-annual evaluation? Let's see it. But let's be careful, colleagues. Parliament must represent all the citizens and those with a majority, even if we are on equal rights, have an additional duty to represent and protect even those who are minorities. The rules must not allow any power to overpower others - that is what balance means - and must not allow those who manage power to exaggerate in its exercise. Let's get modern, yes; let's avoid these risks, okay. But ask yourself why in 1948, following a debate, the referendum was not planned. Were they not the main supporters of the people, of democracy, of vote and of rights? Yet, it was not considered. If we were to hold a proactive referendum on the FTA today, I think it would win the people, but I do not agree with that either, because representative democracy also means preventing groups, lobbying, powers, organizations, companies and organised structures representing a minority from being overwhelmed. In this context, the reduction in the number of MPs can be a small part and can also be shared (the United States has a Senate of 100 people, not of 1,000, then they have a much larger House, certainly). But it is not the number we have to face, but the institutional and constitutional balance that our country must have. We agree on making the system more efficient, giving due importance to the relationship between powers and working better, but we do not want this to be a purely electoral spot. The reduction in the number of MPs has already been rejected by the Italian people, in the last referendum of 2016, but also in the previous referendum of 2006, reforms proposed respectively by the center-left and center-right; so there have already been two votes of the people. I agree with the need to find a solution, but we have the courage to see it as a whole, because the attempts at commercials and attempts at amendment, both to call for a mechanism so well used in budgetary maneuver and also with the simplification decree, risk calling into question the rule of law, rights and representative democracy, which is a guarantee of mediation. Well, we don't want that. [[Applause from the FI-BP Group]]

ParlaMint-IT_2019-02-06-LEG18-Senato-sed-88.ana.u97	Mr President, ladies and gentlemen, representatives of the Government, I believe that this is very easily understood, and that is precisely why I believe it is well known and clear to us: your proposal, the reduction in the number of Members of Parliament, is entirely directed towards precise research, the consolidation of consensus. It is an electoral initiative, it is not a constitutional reform; on the contrary, it uses constitutional reform to introduce a process oriented more towards the consolidation of the electoral consensus than to a different organization of the Republic, to give centrality to the Parliament, to reduce the conflicts between the State, Regions and local authorities, to build a new institutional architecture. Constitutional changes are always particularly important: it is no coincidence that the legislator has assigned them a particular path and has defined them, in the quorum, in the passages and in the referendum, as a noble and important element. And we are well aware that touching on a constitutional reform, even with the reduction in the number of Members, nevertheless determines the weakening of important balances. There is one particularly important one, which strikes the Senate of the Republic even more than the Chamber of Deputies: to increase the number of senators to 200 can risk reducing the capacity of representation towards minorities, can produce the leaving out of these classrooms important pieces of consensus and minorities fundamental for the organization of parliamentary debate and for the credibility of democracy, because the thresholds that are determined here risk being particularly high and not always consistent with the principles of representation, even regional representation, to which the life and organization of the Senate of the Republic is anchored. Ours has always been an initiative aimed at strengthening the centrality, credibility of Parliament and therefore the credibility of democracy as a fundamental element in restoring a new relationship between citizens and institutions, in order to seek new participation in a stronger and more efficient democratic parliamentary Republic. ParlaMint-IT_2019-02-06-LEG18-Senato-sed-88.ana.u99	Mr President, after so many hours of debate on such an important subject, we are wondering whether there is still something that has not been said and I am thinking of asking myself this question: we are talking about reducing the number of Members and many Members have spoken in favour of reducing the number of Members and, in practice, also in favour of reducing the number of senators, but the first question I would like us to answer is: who is the senator today? But also, what are the expectations that - let us call it this - the great institutional machinery, which defines not only Parliament, but Parliament, the Government and all its functions, has towards the Member and the Senator in particular? Look, in recent years, in which I have been in Parliament, the feeling that with greater regret I have been able to live even in the direct experience is the progressive reduction of the weight and value of the things that are done. I am not just referring to things which have already been extensively denounced, such as frequent recourse to urgent decrees. It would be enough, for this reason, some statistical indices that would tell us how many draft laws of parliamentary origin have been approved in this month and how many instead have been the converted decrees-laws: we would immediately see that the decree is no longer an urgent decree, but is an ordinary instrument with which, objectively, the Government is rampant in the field of others. But there are also other things, perhaps less apparent, less showy, that define even more the real underestimation of the role of the parliamentarians. I am thinking, for example, of what happened recently with the decree simplifications and - let me say this - the disaster that also passed to the House, where everything remained untouchable, making the mechanism of bicameralism useless. I am thinking of the cut-off amendments: first, the over-production of the purely governmental branded amendments and, then, the complete cut-off of these amendments. All with a feeling of loosening, which once again poses the problem: but who is the senator? How important is my thinking, my ability to make decisions, my ability to influence processes? I am thinking, for example, of a speech that I have recently made in this House, in which I have put one behind the other - and I have also delivered it - all the questions, motions and questions that have never been answered. [[Applause from the FI-BP Group]] This means that, basically, even the ordinary citizen knows that asking us something, in the light of his problems, hoping for an answer that is a way of influencing decision-making, is a probability close to zero. But that is not how democracy is made: in democracy a correct, honest diagnosis, really full of respect for the democratic truth, would lead us to say: how many mistakes have been made in this progressive devaluation; how much salt has been thrown on the wounds and how much salt has been thrown on the ground so that nothing grew, making in fact very little significant the work, passing the desire to present yet another question, yet another question. This, however, is truly the civil death of Parliament and constitutes a progressive draining of freedom. What we want is exactly the opposite: to put the role and value of the senator and the senate at the centre of attention and debate. We Romans are used to reading, at all corners of the streets, the famous acronym SPQR: Senatus populusque romanus. For us the synthesis of the Senate with the population is imprinted in all the tombstones and signs that define the history of a city, an empire, a country: the Senate and the people. We really have a strong feeling that by falsifying the Senate, in reality, people are shutting their mouths, limiting their capacity for expression and putting their own needs, needs and wealth in front of the construction of a renewed democracy. This is what the country needs: to look with new eyes at a democracy that includes, once again, from what was called the Upper Chamber, the competence on the cultural, historical, legal, economic and linguistic level, that includes people who know what they are talking about and who talk about what they know, with that appropriateness of language that - once again - reflects the ancient lemma of the Romans: Rem tene, verba seqientur (if you know the subject, the words will follow). Sometimes we have the feeling that there is no possibility of expressing the specific competence of so many Members of this House, where there are people who come from a long political history and people who come from a long professional history and it is in the right mix between exquisitely political competence and specifically professional competence that we build the bills that take shape in a Commission and feed on the contributions of the majority and the minority. We know very well that when a bill of parliamentary initiative enters the Commission it will emerge strongly enlivened, enriched and modified by the contributions of many colleagues - of the majority and the opposition - in an exchange that arises from the sharing of deep values and the idea that we do not distinguish ourselves on that subject by political belonging, but we respond to it for the role of representatives of the people. All this will be lost, because in the purely autocratic vision of the government that governs itself and responds only to itself of its own decisions we will lose sight that it is in the division of powers that the profound structure of democracy is built. We cannot allow this interference, nor the reduction of Parliament, because, rightly, as has been said, the question is not in the number of Members, but in the fact that this choice responds to an electoral maneuver and with it we say: gentlemen, in seventy years of Republic, one third of the senators who sat in those classrooms was completely useless and, in the accounts, think about how much you would have spared. But how can such a thing be said? But how can you postulate a futility that you have made possible? This government, in fact, in a single year of life, has given a huge boost to the nullification of parliamentary work: it is you who have made the role of the Senate useless. But we expect the government to pass. This is the beauty of democracy: we expect other times and models to come, within which senators can express all their wealth, competence and political passion, in a synthesis between majority and opposition that takes into account, in one case and another, what is good for the country. [[Applause from the FI-BP Group. Congratulations.]]

ParlaMint-IT_2019-02-06-LEG18-Senato-sed-88.ana.u102	Senator Mallegni is enlisted to speak. You have the right to do so.
ParlaMint-IT_2019-02-06-LEG18-Senato-sed-88.ana.u103	Mr President, the draft constitutional law we are debating today was presented by a dean of the Senate (the decanese is not an offense, on the contrary he would like to be a compliment), who obviously lived, like you, President, the experience of 2005, when the center-right, with the House of Freedom, presented the constitutional project that became law and then, the following year, was - unfortunately, in my opinion - made with little foresight a close confrontation in the country to lead to the disapproval of the measure approved by the Chambers. In the meantime, several years have passed since then - fourteen, at this point - and, with particular reference to the last constitutional amendment, it has been said that in a country like ours we could no longer talk about constitutional changes and that, if the cancellation of the Senate had not occurred at that precise moment, on 4 December 2016, we would never have been able to discuss the constitutional change that could go in the direction we wanted, namely the reduction of the number of parliamentarians. Well, after just two and a half years, we are here: as always, however, not all doughnuts come out with a hole. We should have gone a long way here and agreed on a five-year mandate programme which included reducing the number of parliamentarians, but first of all, on page 1 of the centre-right programme, we had included presidentialism. Since, when we go to touch the Constitution, there are more demanding steps to be taken than the examination of an ordinary law, another opportunity has probably been missed. The presidentialist logic, in fact, is what we - together, as a united centre-right - had agreed with the electoral corps, the Italian citizens, for the previous general elections on 4 March. I do not want to forget that we took 12.147,000 votes, 12.147,000 citizens who had voted for the 103-page programme that the centre-right, after signing it with its leaders, had submitted to them; on page 1 of that programme it was written that we wanted a presidential Republic, so that there would be a President directly elected by the citizens and a Parliament with reduced numbers, of course, as we had already proposed in the constitutional project of 2005, approved by the Chambers. We missed this opportunity, but also another one, because we had not written that we wanted to redesign only the institutional and constitutional structure with the presence of a President elected directly by the people. No; we had also said something else, we had taken into consideration the question of the Provinces which, in our opinion, should have been reintroduced. In this parliamentary term, too, we have tabled draft legislation on this subject, convinced that the Italian Provinces must return to having a President, because this is the first institutional level of serious coordination. Every time I say what I am going to say, some of my colleagues in the majority remind me in the Commission that I have already said so, but I am laughing and seeing that I have been a local administrator for many years and I continue to do so, I will tell you again to the point of exhaustion, because perhaps some of you, who continue to do so, if you forget it: the most important level for a municipality is the provincial one, because in the provincial logic we are talking about the structure of the participating societies and the issue of urban planning. There is no regional regulatory plan; there are some crazy regions, such as Tuscany, that think they can manage everything from the regional scope, therefore Florentinocentric, but it is wrong. We had said, agreed, wrote and signed this pact with the voters that as soon as we had been a government force, we would have carried on the project of the Macroregions, because 20 Regions are an absolute madness, and we would have re-established the Provinces with direct election of the President of the Provincial Council, giving real powers to the mayors and sufficient resources to be able to carry out the activity on the territory. Well, the elephant gave birth to the mouse, because a serious battle could be waged, in the interest of the future of the nation, which could actually lead to a cost reduction, that yes, because instead of 20 Regions there would be five and because we would have given real powers to the provincial council that, in the end, has the same costs as before, since in the meantime, in the logic of the Delrio project that I always opposed, both as administrator and as parliamentarians, the Provinces did not reduce their costs, because the prospect of the shift of staff from the provincial agency to others is resoundingly failed. The Provinces still have the staff but no longer have the functions, they still have the costs but they do not have the receipts. Also on this occasion, in the budget law that was passed, with our proudly opposed vote, before the end of the year, the Provinces had asked us 1.8 billion to try to make investments on schools and roads and 280 million for the current part and we gave them zero for the current and 280 million for the investments (for the series: you understood little). So today we are discussing a matter on which we are opposed? Absolutely not: how could we be against it? We are opposed to the spirit that led us on the road to constitutional change. We had to do ten things and we did half of them. Parliament was first called into the Commissions and then in these days in the Chamber to discuss hours and hours of a measure that could be structured in a presidential way, with the reduction of the number of regions and the redevelopment of the Provinces. 15.04 (Following MALLEGNI). I say, to be even more pleasant, to those who have made a path with us. I look at you, Minister, because there is a replacement going on at the Presidency and then, not knowing who to look at, I turn to the Minister and you know that the Rules of Procedure of the Senate provide for the senator to turn to the Presidency bench (I have been taken up several times by President Calderoli). Let's just say in this passage I was looking at her. PRESIDENT. - The debate is closed. Thank you, Senator, but we were both very careful. MALLEGNI (FI-BP). I believe that in this case too the friends of the League have missed an opportunity and tell President Calderoli I am very sorry, but I have to say it: even in this case you have shortcuts. You have arrived with the lion's long nails and you have returned home with those of the penguin, because, frankly, compared to a constitutional provision with which we had to bring home the results that I recalled just now, one by one (from presidentialism, the Regions, the Provinces, the reduction in the number of parliamentarians), we are content with the reduction of the parliamentary number. Okay. I have often heard this Parliament say that we are doing something in the meantime. Let us do something about pensions, let us do something about income, let us do something about great works, let us do something about it. You know what that means? Don't do anything about anything. [[Applause from the FI-BP Group]] It means making little bits and little bits. So at this point, Mr President, dissatisfaction reigns. I hope that the Italian community realizes the situation and, like us, is deeply dissatisfied with a measure that could be revolutionary and instead it is just a warm cloth in an electoral logic that the government allies think they can use during the European elections. [[Applause from the FI-BP Group]]
ParlaMint-IT_2019-02-06-LEG18-Senato-sed-88.ana.u104	She is registered to speak Senator Faedeli. You have the right to do so.
ParlaMint-IT_2019-02-06-LEG18-Senato-sed-88.ana.u105	Mr President, I must tell you that I have a particular concern. Although I know that we should not address individual senators, I would like to address President Calderoli, whom I see in this House and whom I thank. I was very surprised, Mr Calderoli, but I also say this to the Government, that this measure which proposes constitutional reform has been brought to the attention of the House and that there has not even been (I call it that, I apologize) the respect for this House to illustrate, motivate, argue, try to convince because, in my opinion, we are facing a part that is not trivial, insignificant, is not technical; we are not saying that we are still becoming parliamentarians by subtracting another number from an "X" number. This impressed me very much and made me think. I do not say this to Senator Calderoli because I know it will not be for this reason, but I was struck by the fact that the Government, therefore the majority forces, chose not to come to this House to present a vision, a hypothesis of the operation of the country, its institutions and democracy. You should have put together this part concerning the reduction in the number of Members of Parliament, that on federalism, which you are dealing with elsewhere, once again, without a real parliamentary debate, and the issue of electoral law. However, what worries me most is that you have broken up between the two chambers: here the reduction in the number of parliamentarians and in the House the issue of the referendum, which is the real point of a political vision that you have. So when we have political but legitimate visions, we should come to this House and explain what we want to do in the relationship between the proposed referendum and the legislative function that must remain in the House and the Senate. [[Applause from the PD Group]] This, in fact, is the element that worries me. The break-up you did first of all goes against a story of transparency, responsibility, but also democratic confrontation in the parliamentary classrooms. Not only do we not want to discuss all the proposed measures to amend the Constitution (Senator Collina said so before me and I agree), but outside, there is no discussion in the country. It almost seems that each of these different measures has a value in itself unrelated to the other, thus reducing the importance of these changes. I think that this is an element of non-transparency, of non-political responsibility and had never happened in the history of the Republic. We may not have agreed, not to have seen the other drafts of constitutional change come to an end, or to have advanced others within the different coalitions, but in these classrooms there has always been the vision that was proposed to the Assembly, to the parliamentarians and to the country of the reasons for the changes, of the changes that they wanted to propose. On the other hand, in this House we do not even have an overall view of presenting a single measure, because the same majority in the Senate that has some constitutional amendment measures discussed in the House has another part of the measure discussed and with some regions even the issue of federalism. In my opinion, this is a point that we have the political obligation, even before political parties, to communicate to the country. At this juncture the connection between these measures is being removed from the country and I think this is the first democratic damage in terms of transparency that you have chosen to do, to make everything simple, as if it were a walk. It is not true that these changes do not affect them. I come to deal with two other issues as well. I am very concerned that we are not discussing the two measures together, the one on the agenda here in the Senate and the one under discussion in the House on the proposed referendum. I quote a source I know not to be loved by everyone, but it is always useful to quote it. I think Norberto Bobbio was right when he said that nothing is likely to kill democracy more than the excessive fake democracy and populism, exactly what is happening with the proposed referendum. There it opens up to partial lobbying, there is no clarity on matters but above all there is no clarity on the legislative function: whether or not it will remain to those who will be elected to the House and the Senate, considering the laws that will be presented by the so-called - fundamental term that lies in our Constitution - people. Colleagues, this is the emptying of the elements of democratic function. I think this is the subject on which we must all think together and reflect because, in my opinion, it is worth, even in our internal debate, knowing that this connection exists, that it is a vision of democratic emptying that all the speeches of the Democratic Party have demonstrated. We are not prepared to support it because for us the democratic relationship between the elected and those who obviously elect, with a reasonable number of reports, remains fundamental, but above all the democratic function of the legislation, therefore the legislative rules that are made only in the parliamentary fora. This is a basic principle of liberal democracy; it is fundamental from this point of view, as well as being the foundation of our Constitution. I do not list the issues under discussion, which from my point of view are also unconstitutional. We'll see about that. At the same time, however, there is also a political fact of method. I say this because in this very House a part of us in the previous legislature has lived on constitutional reforms a relationship between majority and minority with a political choice, because we choose confrontation, mediation. It is not something that happens if you agree or not, that is, if the minority agrees with me then I listen to it, otherwise I do not listen to it. The choice of a political mediation is a precise choice that is made. Too bad that in this House and in the Commission, for example, we do not choose to listen to the amending proposals that the oppositions have put forward. This is also an element of the political assessment of this measure. I want to understand why the House is having a discussion between the majority and the minority; there are debates and receptions - right or wrong I remain of the opinion I have expressed - but there is a dynamic relationship, as it is right that it is on constitutional reforms, between majority and opposition Here no. Here, Mr President - I am telling you explicitly because it would be worth telling President Alberti Casellati - in the first Commission we found ourselves faced with the fact that the President of the Commission considered the proposals we have made as a matter of fact - following a choice which, however, is made of constitutional reform, a reduction in the number of Members - as regards the composition, functions, and the consistent and consequent changes, from our point of view, to the legislative text presented. To declare that there is extraneousness of matter - I would like to stress this - means exactly that our proposals do not interest him, that they do not even want to take into consideration them, not even the one we think so popular that should be taken into account, that is, the demand for the same active and passive electorate base between the House and the Senate. To declare quite frankly that even such a proposal is out of the question when changing the number of Members of Parliament, I consider it really strange. Not to mention the fact that from our point of view we should think of the presence of self-governments. This is another point of denial of the relationship between majority and minority when discussing constitutional issues. We have never used it; we have not done so in the previous parliamentary term, no one has ever used it; rather, on every constitutional change a relationship has been sought because it is known that the relationship between majority and minority has political value. I believe that we will have to have a full debate on this before we reach the final vote on the measure. [[Applause from the PD Group]]
