ParlaMint-IT_2019-02-06-LEG18-Senato-sed-88.ana.u107	Mr President, ladies and gentlemen, I would also like to address the Minister: I will speak with the Undersecretary, but I would have liked to have the Minister here for relations with Parliament and direct democracy. I quote the full name of your delegation, because I think it relates to what we are discussing today. Already some commentators, immediately after the birth of the Government, had highlighted and noticed that it was almost an oxymoron to speak of Minister for Relations with Parliament and Minister for Direct Democracy. Parliament is in fact the emblem, the temple or the instrument - use the noun you think most useful - of representative democracy. This definition of your delegation, Minister, which is not - but I hope they will tell you this - leads you to have relations with Parliament, which is not very frequent since you are not even here at this time, but you are also pushing for direct democracy. We should not have been surprised, even if the oxymoron is all there is to it, because in many political statements that we have heard there were signs in this regard. At the beginning of the last parliamentary term I recall that the arrival of the 5 Star Movement in Parliament had been greeted by saying that Parliament would be opened like a can of tuna. There has always been in the speeches of the leaders of Movimento 5 Stars the need to overcome representative democracy and then we have also reached the hypothesis of the draw of parliamentarians. I would therefore like to ask the Minister what relations the Government and the Minister for relations with Parliament have with Parliament in this parliamentary term. We have had some very significant examples, and the most striking one, which really is a uniform in Parliament's history, is the last budget law. The fact that we can't even read the maxi amendment and then get to vote on a budget law whose content neither the opposition parliamentarians nor the majority parliamentarians knew about it was really a disgusting incident. Moreover, having some time to read the measure, mistakes could also have been avoided, which were then recognized by the same majority. I quote two for everyone: the so-called goodness tax, namely the doubling of the Irpef rate for the Onlus and voluntary associations and the failure to continue the tax exemption for those who have been hit by the collapse of the Morandi bridge. These are two things that the government has admitted to have to make up for, but maybe, if there was time to read, maybe we could have avoided mistakes, because then it takes time to make up for it. Let us also think of the so-called simplification decree: we have spent hours and days without even being able to understand the timetable of work, with senators in fact held hostage, without respecting the time, dignity and sense that must have the work done in the parliamentary classrooms. These are signs that we must not forget. So, by addressing my colleagues in the majority who are listening to me, the real issue is what you want to do with representative democracy and what you think about democracy. On this subject too there is a series of clues. In many statements we hear you say that you represent 60 million Italian citizens. Those who govern should really govern, thinking of the 60 million Italian citizens, which I do not think is always being done. But it does not represent 60 million Italians; representation is what we find in this Parliament, which is most composed. The President-in-Office of the Council proclaims himself a "people's lawyer." On the TAV I heard the Minister of Infrastructure say that the Government is sovereign; in fact, the TAV was approved by Parliament, because it is an international treaty and, in the current Constitution, it is Parliament that is sovereign in this case. Also on the case of the Diciotti ship, on which the Junta of Parliamentary Elections and Immunitys will have to speak, the issue is not whether Minister Salvini should be judged or whether it should be the whole Government that puts the memory. The theme is: Can who rules be over the laws? Can the political decision be above the laws? I quote these clues because, in my opinion, they are indicative of the type and quality of democracy that this majority wants to represent. There are weights and counterweights in the Constitution. There is a focus on the relationship between the Commission and Parliament. And look at that, who in the course of history told the Government "I represent the people, I am the people," in Italy did not work very well; let's not forget it. Democracy serves. What's on your mind? Is there a plebiscite model? I do not hide from you that I, like my previous colleague, am concerned about this parallel debate. We are discussing the reduction in the number of Members and the House is talking about a reform of the proposed referendum. This proposal will in fact give rise to a very strong contrast between Parliament's legislative function and that which could generate this type of referendum. From all these indications, which give me much concern, do I assume that there is no need for reform? No, of course; we need reforms. The Constitution is not untouchable; indeed, in the Constitution itself it is inherent in the way and path in which it can be updated. The debate on parliamentary reforms is, or should be, a high moment in the parliamentary debate. As you know, we ourselves committed ourselves, in the last parliamentary term, to a complex constitutional reform, with many open issues; this reform was then rejected in the referendum. This is to say that we feel the need for reforms; it is not a desire to repudiate this need, we do not reject what we have done. We have not won the referendum, but we believe that constitutional reforms are necessary, just as we believe that democracy serves and is important. But it needs to be updated. Among the amendments that we have tabled and which have not been accepted in the Commission were again - because you know that it is our point of view - overcoming the perfect bicameralism, because it is necessary for the times of democracy to be reduced, for democracy to become effective and to respond quickly. We all know that the identical and equal shuttle between the House and the Senate of the measures is antihistorical and certainly does not help us to be credible as an institution. But we cannot talk about this. Just as we are not talking about federalism with principles of solidarity, we are not talking about the balance and the counterweights between powers or the excess of urgent decree, which is another anomaly of the situation; all this is not reflected in the proposal. What is it? There is only a reduction in the number of Members. It is a gracious scalp, I understand it well; it can be wielded very well in the electoral campaign. But you do not pose important problems, because emptying representative democracy does not open up to the power of the people, as you say, but it opens up to the risk that lobbying will determine the choices; and this is a serious problem for democracy. Of course, I am convinced that this measure has a particularly electoral interest and that is why you have put nothing else. I already have almost in my ears the slogans with which you can say 'here', the Democratic Party has spoken against, because they are the party of the caste, because they are attached to the armchairs' and I say only this because I am generally a moderate person, but I expect much more. Well, I say to you today in this House, imagining that you will wield this scalp in the electoral campaign: please, at least this time, do not be so dishonest. The Democratic Party is for the reduction of parliamentarians; we would not have strongly wanted that constitutional reform, if not so, but we are not pretending to be a facade unanimity on something that is not a reform, but it is simply a perhaps smart move, but that we hope the citizens can understand and judge as such. [[Applause from the PD Group]]
ParlaMint-IT_2019-02-06-LEG18-Senato-sed-88.ana.u108	He's enlisted to speak Senator Cangini. You have the right to do so.
ParlaMint-IT_2019-02-06-LEG18-Senato-sed-88.ana.u109	Mr President, I would also like to point out that even if members of the government, my fellow senators, were deprived of their status and cross-referenced the number of Members of Parliament with that of the population of the Member States, Italy would place itself at European level at 22nd place out of 28. So, at least in this case, unlike usual, we're not the taillight and we're not the evil exception. Of course, we can discuss everything and therefore we can also discuss the possibility of reducing the number of Members. It has been done so many times in the past: the first time it was done in the framework of the work of the Commission chaired by the Liberal Aldo Bozzi (it was 1983), then there were the Commission Iotti-De Mita, the bicameral D'Alema, the organic reform of the Constitution launched by the government Berlusconi, rejected by the voters with the referendum of 2006, and the reform Renzi-Boschi, rejected by the voters in 2016. The precedents are, but they are of different seed: in all these cases it was, precisely, organic reforms of the Constitution, the powers of the State and the form of government within which there was also discussion of the desirability of reducing the number of parliamentarians, but it was a debate on the sidelines, marginal, almost irrelevant to the scope of those reforms. In this case this is not the case: the draft constitutional law we are talking about today has as its object only the reduction of the number of parliamentarians. One wonders what the logic is: is it perhaps to make politics stronger and more authoritative? Is it to make Parliament's work more effective and efficient, or - as we say today and it is a good thing that we say so, even if it is often done in bad faith - to support the concept of the primacy of politics? Obviously not: the logic is exactly the opposite, that is to degrade politics, because the only argument to credit this reform, in the narrative of the parties that support the government, is the economic one: the money taken from the caste. Still with the ancient spirit, still with the narrative of politics as a matter of crime, privileges and money. That is, if you believe that this system can fill the gap - and it would be foolish to deny - between citizens and Parliament, between citizens and politics, between citizens and the Palace, you deceive yourself. If you had read some history books you would know that the outcome will surely be the opposite: from Robespierre to Matteo Renzi, those who intended to justify constitutional reforms or reforms of any kind with this figure and with this key reading did not end well. We still remember: the reform of the Senate - hear, hear - will save us a billion, said the then president of the Council Matteo Renzi. In a clear political difficulty Emmanuel Macron is trying to accredit a similar reform on the number of MPs represented at the Elysée, but these are small tricks. So you continue to feed the beast of anti-politics, feed it with steaks dripping the blood of the political class and the beast will fatally torment you too. In a country that spends more than 800 billion every year it is ridiculous and even a little dangerous to go and try to make spending economy from the institutions and from those constitutionally guaranteed bodies on which depends the functionality of our democracy. But perhaps it is not a coincidence, indeed it is certainly not, because Minister Fraccaro, who I see intent to write to the computer, certainly said it with an admirable intellectual clarity: the two measures go hand in hand, that is the reduction of the number of parliamentarians is accompanied by the measure that wants to introduce the proposed referendum in this country. The consequence of the proposed referendum, as it was thought, of course, will be to completely annihilate Parliament, to completely deconstruct the state, to completely marginalize politics. For you 500,000 signatures, or rather 500,000 clicks on a digital platform, will be worth more than the vote of millions of voters. It is clear that this destroys democracy and delegates itself to a few, driven by the interests of very few, legislative power, because it is clear that this system, if it were really introduced into our system, would be a splendid service to lobbyists and interest groups. What would you do if you produced weapons in the Trompia Valley? I personally would wait for the first fact of black news (made of black news, unfortunately, they have always existed and continue to exist, in our country). Is there a robbery with massacres in a Triveneto mansion? Very well, the next day I would invest a few tens of thousands of euros in a social campaign, I would easily collect 500,000 signatures and the liberalisation of the sale of firearms would be law in this country. So for the interests of the tobacco lobby, for the interests of the gambling lobby, for the interests, clearly well represented in this House, as in the Chamber of the House, of the web giants. So, do you really want to make legislative power available to lobbyists and small but powerful interest groups? Think about it, if you don't do it out of the sense of institutions, out of respect for the state, at least do it out of survival instinct. It is clear that, if this instrument were already in the availability of Parliament and the political class, we in Forza Italia would take a moment to collect 500,000 signatures on a law to introduce immediately the TAV, or to immediately launch the flat tax, which the League obviously forgot and which disappeared from the horizon of this Government. And what would happen to your government if these referendums were successful, how, of course, would that happen? Divided as it is on these two points, it would fall fatally. It is not, therefore, a way of proceeding respectful of Parliament, politics and the institutions, but it is a way of proceeding that is independent of politics, regardless of Parliament, regardless of the institutions. In this House last week - it seems to me that it was Wednesday - an important speech was made. The League Leader, Senator Romeo, followed by the Leader of the Movimento 5 Stars, Senator Patuanelli, spoke of the primacy of politics. It is a broad, profound concept which is rarely used, especially in the parliamentary classrooms. At that concept I was initiated many years ago by a gentleman who was a politician, even if he thought that politics was not a profession, but an art, by a gentleman who, when writing the word "political" on a blank sheet, wrote it with a capital "P." That gentleman's name was Francesco Cossiga. I wonder what Francesco Cossiga would say in the face of your proposal to reduce the number of parliamentarians and in the face of your proposal to introduce the proposed referendum, what would he say about your rhetoric about the primacy of politics, about the first Italians, about the first Italians, about the first state, when everything you do and propose by formal acts of government clearly goes in the opposite direction, that is, destroying politics, destroying institutions and consequently, since the force of politics is the strength of States, destroying the credibility, the authority and the strength of the Italian State. If you do not want to stop, then, accompany this reform with a vision - which would already be something - but possibly with a broader vision. Do we want to reduce the number of Members? Let us do this, but at the same time let us put the problem of the lack of representation that this reduction would pose: less parliamentarians, less contacts between the territories and the palace. Then we elect directly the President of the Republic or the Head of Government (I prefer the first, but we can also discuss the second). Do you want to reduce the number of parliamentarians? ParlaMint-IT_2019-02-06-LEG18-Senato-sed-88.ana.u120	Senator Quagliarello is enlisted to speak. You have the right to do so.
ParlaMint-IT_2019-02-06-LEG18-Senato-sed-88.ana.u121	Mr President, Minister, ladies and gentlemen, I am the presenter of a proposal for a law on the reduction of the number of Members of Parliament, already in the last parliamentary term, and then repeated in this parliamentary term, which is basically a consistent copy of the reform presented by the Government, at least of its first version. What is the source of this initiative? I believe that it arises from a shared analysis, at least in the fundamental aspects, beyond tone, from the fact that we are experiencing a period of deep crisis of parliamentary representation. This is not the first time that this has happened in history. It happened in the past, at the beginning of the last century, when mass parties were born, extra-parliamentary parties (not because they were extremes, but because they were outside Parliament). It happened in the 1930s, when it was believed that authoritarian regimes could become normal. It happened with the drifting Assemblyists of the Second World War, and then also with the assembly of the Sixty-eight. Even then there was talk of a crisis of civilization, referring to the crisis of representation. I believe that the crisis we are experiencing today is deeper and even more significant, because of two structural elements. The first is that representation, representative democracy, needs a time of sedimentation; when a representative of the people is given the mandate, he is also given freedom of action and the fact is determined that a judgment on his work will be given after a certain period of time. This justifies the ban on mandatory mandates in representative democracies. Now this time of sedimentation has unfortunately failed. He failed for polls, failed for talk shows, and eventually failed for the network and social networks. Representative democracy also implies the need for self-content, both on the part of the representatives and on the part of the representatives. You can't think of being omniscient, you can't have the fatal presumption of talking about everything, because the law of numbers, which is the basis of democracy, doesn't fit everything. There are some subjects that need special skills. You see, Minister, in this House, until a few legislatures ago, there were those who talked about health and those who spoke about foreign policy; there were few colleagues who spoke about everything. Evidently we are at another time. Now, if all this is true and so we start from a basis of common awareness, yet we are facing a crossroads: do we want to reform this representative democracy in crisis or do we want to destroy it? My intention and intent of the law that I have presented is to reform representative democracy, because, despite everything and despite its imperfections, basically a better tool for governing complex societies is not there. It wasn't produced by history and it wasn't produced by the lucubrations about the future that some visionaries propose to us in some cases. So what is the point of cutting the number of parliamentarians for those who cultivate a reform perspective? To increase the efficiency of Parliament; to streamline the work and thus to allow that time of sedimentation that has failed; to improve the quality of representation in order to recover competence. I believe that these are the basic objectives. On the other hand, those who want to destroy representative democracy think of the reduction in the number of parliamentarians with contempt, even more than towards Parliament, towards the parliamentarians and the political class: even in the propaganda that I read on the net I see the fact that parliamentarians are today considered a kind of metastasis. It follows that the aim is to reduce the damage: not to try, to improve the quality of the damage, but to reduce the damage - the existence of the parliamentarians - to the point of probably tending to zero, because basically there is a culture of suspicion. A Member of Parliament is a person to be suspicious of. I am fully aware that these sentiments are also widespread in civil society, and they are not only present in this House. But I believe that these have been, even in the past, the premises for that fatal presumption of being better that authoritarian or even totalitarian regimes have been fed in history. Where, then, are we going to serve the difference between these two perspectives? In the discussion on some fundamental aspects, on which we hope the two approaches can approach: firstly, we will have to ensure that the criterion of territorial representativeness is also ensured by the cut of Members; secondly, that minorities are guaranteed; thirdly, that there is a positive interaction with other constitutional reforms. On the first two points it would be very important to talk about the cut of parliamentarians together with a proposal for an electoral law, because that is how we would understand whether minorities are guaranteed and whether territories are represented anyway. On the third aspect, what you are proposing to us at the moment is simply an interaction with the proposed referendum. You see, Minister, I have no negative prejudice on this issue, and I believe that even a proactive referendum can help to integrate in some way a representativeness that has more and more difficulty in asserting itself. For me it is therefore not a taboo and I must tell you that I also appreciated some changes that were made compared to the first text, which basically did not hold and made water from all sides. But there are still many aspects that we will have to deal with and that I hope can be examined in an "interactive" way, through a comparison and without barricades. First of all, a proactive referendum cannot even be thought of for spending laws, because obviously this would make our country, at a time when we are serving the crisis of public debt, very weak. You will be aware of the work of the Commission which I had the honour of chairing: it was the first aspect we highlighted. Secondly, it is necessary to limit the number of proposals, because without a limitation it would lead to the substantial repeal of Parliament. Finally, we need to think more about the relationship between the referenda initiative and the legislative initiative of the Chambers, because it is only sketched out in the proposal under discussion. We will be dealing with the debate on the referendum in the next period. And it is clear that it will also depend on the attitude on the text that is being discussed in this House today. I repeat to you, Minister, that as far as I am concerned we will be unprejudiced and we will be ready to cooperate, even if there is a desire for cooperation on your part, but we will be very hard if, on the other hand, this will serve to foment another page, a bit provincial, of anti-parliamentaryism. If all this falls to the level of a proposal of small provincial boulangers, without even the charm of a ride, in this case we will not give you discounts. [[Applause from the FI-BP Group]]

ParlaMint-IT_2019-02-06-LEG18-Senato-sed-88.ana.u124	Senator Astorre is enlisted to speak. You have the right to do so.
ParlaMint-IT_2019-02-06-LEG18-Senato-sed-88.ana.u125	Mr President, the issue of reducing the number of Members has always been considered within a broader reform project. Since the Bozzi reform, since the reform project approved in the last legislature by our Government, and rejected by the popular vote of 4 December 2016, the reduction in the number of parliamentarians has always been linked to the need to improve the functioning of the two Chambers. Thinking of changing the Constitution by stopping exclusively at numbers, it seems to me to respond more to a punitive logic towards the political class, in the wake of the most vicious anti-political, and above all to the need to recover electoral consensus from the 5 Stars, than to the desire to overcome the elements that do not allow, in full respect of the representation of the voters and the representativeness of Parliament, the two branches of Parliament to function more efficiently and more rationally. Personally, I consider it absurd to sacrifice our Constitution and our democratic institutions on the altar of the yet important electoral consensus of the European elections. In fact, this seems to me to be the end of this reform, even if it is not too elusive. The draft law under consideration, as it is designed, worsens and greatly worsens the problem concerning the link between the elected and the elector, between the elected and the territory of reference. On the other hand, the fall in the number of parliamentarians considerably reduces the ratio between the number of voters and elected: there is a decrease of more than 35%, however with important problems in smaller regions such as Molise or with fewer inhabitants such as Sardinia. However, beyond that, we all know that to restore that link of representation, that trust between the elected and the electorate and the territory of reference, whose loss is the real protagonist of the breakdown between politics and citizens, are the electoral laws and techniques of transformation of votes into seats. This reform plan also lacks this aspect. Election laws that do not allow, either through preferences or through small single-name colleges (as was the Mattarellum), to directly choose their representatives are the real fall between elected and elected. Leaving unchanged the distinction concerning the age of passive and active electorate in the House as in the Senate is the other great vulnus of this bill. In fact, the problem of the functioning of the Chambers resulting from the discrepancy of the active electorate - eighteen years in the Chamber and twenty-five years in the Senate - is not overcome. This reform is like Fr Abbondio: she lacks courage, she is fearless; she does not address the problem of the functioning of bicameralism; she does not address the problem of the identical functions of the two Chambers; she does not address the problem of the representation of the Chambers on a different active electorate. This reform would make sense and would certainly be approved by all if it could address the real issue of the problem that exists in Italy: the problem of two Chambers that, since 1948, for well-defined historical reasons, do both the same thing; a useless ornament to the functioning of democracy that is overcome with the subterfuge of the armored trust in one Chamber and with the text unchanged to the other House. [[Applause from the PD Group]]
ParlaMint-IT_2019-02-06-LEG18-Senato-sed-88.ana.u126	Senator Misiani is enlisted to speak. You have the right to do so.
ParlaMint-IT_2019-02-06-LEG18-Senato-sed-88.ana.u127	Mr President, I think that no group in this House questions the need for a reform of Parliament's structure. It is from 15 April 1983, when the Commission, chaired by Mr Bozzi, was set up, which followed, after a parliamentary term, bicameral committees, initiatives and referendums aimed at revising the Constitution and, in this context, a reform of Parliament. There is no doubt that there are many open issues and that Parliament is called upon to debate them in order to reform itself. First of all, there is the perfect bicameral arrangement, which objectively represents an anomaly of the Italian Parliament, which - unique in the European Union between the 28 Community countries - provides for absolute equality between the House and the Senate. In 15 Community countries there is a single-cameral system and in the other 12 a two-cameral system, but with a Chamber linked to the Government by a trust relationship and the other Chamber generally representative of territorial autonomy. We should discuss a different balance between the Government and Parliament in the legislative initiative. The statistics of the 17th legislature are eloquent, from this point of view: 379 laws have been adopted, but in 74 percent of the cases they are measures of government initiative; moreover, in 83 cases they are laws of conversion of decrees-laws, which - as is well known - are now an example of alternate monocameralism, because they are really discussed in a House and, then, the bill of conversion is effectively ratified in the other branch of Parliament. We should discuss a different balance between the direction and control role of the House and the Senate and that of legislative production, in a country suffering from an excess of legislative production and a weak role of Parliament in the direction and control of the Commission's work. We should discuss a different link between the work of this House and the work of the Commission, which is indeed a matter of regulation, but it is undoubtedly constitutionally important because, as is well known, the Constitution recognises the role and function of the parliamentary committees. Colleagues, all these issues should be reflected in a wide-ranging, in-depth and meditated reflection on the role of Parliament in our democracy, as well as on the future of Italian democracy, at a time when so many - too many, if I may be allowed - question the value of representative democracy and call for participatory democracy to prevail over representative institutions. I believe that participatory institutions in our democracy must be strengthened, but in the parliamentary assemblies we see initiatives that go in a direction that we consider unbalanced from the point of view of the relationship of representative institutions with the demands that are directly expressed by the electoral body. We should discuss in depth the overall structure of the Republican institutions, within which we should include a reform of Parliament. What is this majority doing, namely the 5 Star Movement, the League and the Government supported by them? This majority is humiliating Parliament. We saw this in the discussion of the budget law for 2019, when, for the first time, the competent standing committee, first in the Senate and then in the House, was not allowed to vote even one amendment. The budget law was passed in a distorted version compared to the version originally presented, with blows of confidence first to the Senate and then to the House of Deputies. You are humiliating Parliament outside the parliamentary classrooms, with an anti-parliamentary rhetoric that humiliates the function of the representatives of the citizens and that is unbearable in a democracy such as we want to defend and protect. You are carrying out a reform to bits and bites, which overall wants to empty Parliament's role. This is in fact the point of the proposal on the proposed referendum, which has been dropped, but in fact an integral part of a draft which provides for the mere reduction in the number of Members. Mr President, this is not the case. This is not the path to an organic and meditated reform of Parliament. This is not the way to give Italian democracy a Parliament that is placed in a position to carry out fully the functions that the Constitution itself assigns to it. Let us discuss together how we can seriously change Parliament, not with proposals which seem to be detached from each other, but with an organic plan, which deals with the problems I referred to earlier, those which have been unresolved since 1983. Let us discuss together how to change Parliament in order to improve our democracy. [[Applause from the PD Group]]

Changing the composition of the elective Assemblies achieves an accomplished sense only if this idea is framed in a broader context of reform of the Constitution, which takes all these aspects into account. So let's talk about reducing the number of MPs and senators, but let's discuss the reform of bicameralism or rationalization of the form of government. ParlaMint-IT_2019-02-06-LEG18-Senato-sed-88.ana.u145	Mr President, most of my colleagues have already spoken, even in a comprehensive way, and they have well represented the idea and position of the Democratic Party in relation to the measure under discussion. I too would like to leave a few comments briefly in a few minutes, because constitutional reforms - as many of my colleagues have already said - have been on the agenda in Italy for over twenty-five years, even if they have never been completed. So we know that the problem is there and we share it: we too have tried. But, with the exception of the revision of Title V of the second part of the Constitution, all other attempts have failed. We therefore know that there is this need, because there is no doubt that there is a need for institutional reform; there is no doubt that there is a problem of the efficiency of Parliament, as well as of the stability of governments; there is also no doubt that there is an increase - it has been said several times - of the need for citizens to participate in the lives of our society and our institutions. But the need for citizens' participation and the need to build a political system that better interprets and represents the needs of citizens cannot start from a lie and an answer that is only a facade. The point to be introduced is how we deal with it, how we approach a reform of a complex system such as that of our democracy. The question cannot be asked only in numerical terms of greater or less efficiency, or in terms of cost reduction, hiding an elementary principle, that is, the issue - which has also been mentioned several times - of the representativeness of Members. This issue is directly concerned with the quality of our democratic system and, consequently, with the principle of popular sovereignty. So already focusing on the content of this measure, in the face of the characteristics and requirements of our order, emerges the lack, the senselessness and the arbitrariness. It is an institutional reform which exclusively concerns the reduction of the number of senators and Members without redefining in any way the tasks and functions of Parliament; without leaving the perfect bicameralism; without updating the division of competence with the Regions (very important), which does not touch at all on the issue of federalism, which should also be of interest to someone in this House. A reform that does not want to improve our democracy, but only aims to send out a message - yet again for the truth - demagogic, populist and only in electoral terms. For example, as some Members have rightly pointed out earlier in this House: why two hundred senators and four hundred Members? What is the criterion for achieving this figure? What vision is there in this determination? What impact does it have on the reduction of the work of the Commissions and Parliament on legislative procedures, on the representativeness of small territories and small municipalities as well? Has this been dealt with even in a small part? We have said that and we have also tried to do so in the past: we are not against the reduction in the number of Members. But reducing parliamentarians without an overall reform of the Constitution is a simple spot, to use the term that many of my colleagues have used and which makes good. It does not serve democracy and it does not solve any problems. And the idea of changing our democratic system by reducing only a few seats in Parliament is truly repulsive. Last legislature, the Democratic Party proposed a reform, the guiding principle of which was the overcoming of equal bicameralism. We believe that it is necessary to start from this, i.e. from the need to overcome an arrangement in which Chamber and Senate, elected with different criteria, perform the same functions. We have tabled amendments to this measure. Let's discuss this. We want to make our contribution. We think that this is the perspective that allows for greater efficiency and quality of the work of the Chambers. I would ask for permission to hand over the remainder of my speech so that it can be annexed to the Minutes of the sitting. [[Applause from the PD Group]]

ParlaMint-IT_2019-02-06-LEG18-Senato-sed-88.ana.u148	Mr President, Minister, ladies and gentlemen, Parliament is the legislative body of the State and therefore the most sacred place of a rule of law. In Parliamentary classrooms, laws are born that affect the lives of citizens. The Parliamentary Assembly is the highest essence of democracy. Forza Italia has always been in favour of reducing the number of members of Parliament, but precisely because it is deeply aware of the fundamental importance of the balance of powers within the rule of law, it has always conceived it in a framework of overall reform of the state structure. If we decide to intervene on the legislative power, the father of democratic representation, we must do so by intervening, equally and at the same time, also on the executive power. Mr Fraccaro, I look at you because it is to you that my speech is addressed. Any attempt to reform Parliament, which was also subject to a referendum, had foreseen a reduction in the number of Members. The last, on the initiative of the Renzi Government, saw a Senate with different functions from the Chamber and a different balance of powers. The previous reform, in 2005, also submitted to referendums with negative results, was that of the Berlusconi Government. However, it was a very far-reaching reform, which was drawn up after a long and thorough parliamentary discussion. A reform measure created through a precise constitutional architecture, which supported a wide change, truly articulated and comprehensive, within which the substantial reduction in the number of parliamentarians found a complete logic. I repeat: we agree with the reduction in the number of Members, provided that this is done within a more comprehensive design, in which the burdens and counterweights that are the basis of democratic rules are redefined. Now - instead - a very original but also very risky path is being taken. On the one hand, in the Senate, we approve a constitutional bill that reduces the number of senators from 315 to 200 and of deputies from 630 to 400. In the other branch of Parliament there is a constitutional proposal in which the proposed referendum is introduced, which would have validity, where approved by the Italians, even without a definite quorum. In other words, an active minority could prevail over the majority of Italians, and that - in my opinion - is the denial of democracy. It is clear, therefore, that there is a lack of constitutional engineering which holds together the two reforms proposed by the current majority. Furthermore, there are serious dangers to our democracy, because the rules concerning the other powers of the State remain unchanged, in fact creating an obvious and risky imbalance. It was not by chance that, in writing our beautiful Constitution, the constituent Fathers held in extreme account that none of the powers could prevail over the other. Therefore, while we are in favour of reducing the number of Members, we have strong doubts that the reforms taken without taking into account the fact that a democratic state must have as its absolute priority the freedom of its citizens, who have rights, first of all that of being represented. Parliament must best represent the different interests, political objectives and views that coexist in our citizenship and must be able to do so properly, coherently and consciously. In the text you have decided to further expand the scissors between elected and inhabitants: how do you think it is possible to carry out the real exercise of the representative function? This proposal of yours risks creating serious problems of representation of the smallest regions, such as the Valle d'Aosta, Umbria, Molise, Basilicata and Sardinia, which would run the serious risk of not having elected any candidate, especially the Senate, where, taking into account the small number of representatives, we could have elected only in the most populous regions, with the highest number of inhabitants. Another organizational consideration: the work of the parliamentarians is also based on collegial work in the Commissions, on the division of labour, on specialization; and the Commissions institutionalize precisely this need. We cannot, therefore, want to halve the number of parliamentarians and think that the quality of the laws is not affected. This would also require real constitutional reform, with the overcoming of our perfect bicameralism. The operation of the current system has been slow and cumbersome, in comparison especially with the other systems, where the second Chambers generally deal with territorial issues. He quoted a famous opinion from the judge of the Supreme Court of the United States of America, Felix Frankfurter, about the law, which must be based on something much deeper and more plausible than personal preference, having to be based on fundamental foundations rooted in history. The problem is that there has been no intervention of yours that has linked - in this House of the Italian Parliament - the world that is changing and therefore attention to this perspective, with respect to the history that we have. But does it seem possible that we are about to adopt such an important constitutional reform without any of you trying to link the history and vision of a new world, to link our Parliament and the new world? Is that an act of respect for this House? [[Applause from the PD Group]] Learning democracy means having memory of previous parliamentary work, because if there is no memory here, it is clear that there is none in the country. And this is the place where President Napolitano, accepting - extraordinarily - the renewal of his position as President of the Republic, said that it was essential that the parliamentary institutions and the relationship between Parliament and Government should be reformed, because without that radical change it is not that he would not have agreed to be President, but Italy would have remained back in the world, would not have adapted its institutions to a primary function that the same institutions have, that is, allow the country to progress. We must remember what happened and you cannot fail to consider two years of work in the last legislature. I also address Mr Fraccaro, with whom I have shared several years in the Committee on Constitutional Affairs in the House. We have discussed this with democratic attention and respect for the matter that we have been dealing with that cannot be left out in this case too, Minister; it cannot be left out of your intentions. We have discussed memory; we have discussed introducing a function, to the new Senate, which was called an assessment of the impact of public policies, which should be one of your first objectives, for what you say, namely to assess exactly what happens in the lives of citizens and on territories after Parliament takes a decision. Nothing, no trace of correlation between your design and that reasoning, which was a reasoning even before a center-left choice. There is no trace of the memory of the autonomy and I address my colleagues of the League: is it ever possible that in one country, in which it is more normal to resort to one another rather than to compete towards the same objectives (and I say this as a Lombard) there is no trace of the discussion that for seventy years, with great cultural peaks, this country has made on the relationship between local institutions and Parliament? [[Applause from the PD Group]] Is it possible that there is not a voice of yours that says how this vision also includes a design of distribution and diffusion of power and responsibility and therefore of strengthening autonomy? All this is not there and I believe that it is a great limit of your work, as a great limit lies in the attitude that I have already mentioned, of colleagues who are facing this as a bureaucratic passage, as if there were no obligation to cover some of the issues that I have introduced and all the debate that took place over two years of work, in the last parliamentary term, in which all the parliamentary groups participated anyway. Not to mention the previous editions, through bicameral commissions or other, which have faced and tried to review and update the democratic culture of the country; they have done so in one direction or another, but have tried. Colleagues, you have the attitude of those who are here by chance, or who simply live this as a bureaucratic formality, or who do not have the tension that leads each of us to study endlessly, because the respect that we owe to this Assembly is the respect that we owe to our country. [[Applause from the PD Group]] The respect we owe to this House is respect for our history. But do you remember what they said when they were in exile, Sturzo and Salvemini, just before our Constitution was written? They spoke of the centrality of the development of the human person, of the universalist system of our rights and of how slowly the institutional power should spread, so that the written rights in the first part of our Constitution would be universally guaranteed. Of all this we should have respect and also of what has happened in the stories of the people who wrote the Constitution and who have continued to debate over the decades. Among the most curious things that I happened to learn about in the work done for the last constitutional reform, there was an anecdote that impressed me: many communists, many members of the Communist Party, arrived in Parliament without knowing anything, even struggling to speak Italian. The Communist Party asked the most experienced, culturally competent people and at that time more prepared than the Christian Democracy to give lectures to communist parliamentarians. Can we therefore think of dealing with such a significant change in the Constitution without taking into account the tension of change, the desire to give the best of themselves that taught us, in that case, the representatives of two political forces, who were exactly the opposite in the parliamentary deployment? Can we do that? No, because otherwise we would do nothing but break a relationship of trust, which can only stand in the background between the components and the political parties that sit in Parliament, when the change of the Constitution is at stake. The relationship of trust is to think that, even though we have different positions, we can achieve a common goal together. So, all the arguments I've brought in lead you to rethink how we deal with this issue. All the motivations and also the personal stories and cultural contributions that I have tried briefly to remember induce you to bring, with your head high, dignity in these classrooms, also because, otherwise, I have the impression that we would find ourselves - I say it in particular to our colleagues of the Movimento 5 Stars - in front of another mocking act, similar to the Porcellum, which would be a terrible and very serious blow for our democracy. The debate in this House took place, just as it was held regularly, for a certain period, in the Commission. Therefore, we are in the most ordinary management of the measure. These days I have heard very often about the need for comprehensive reform and not just on time, not being able to change a single point of the Constitution if everything else is not changed. Colleagues, I no longer fall for it, because I fell for it, as Minister, in 2005: after having voted on the reform that changed the whole Constitution, it was rejected by the popular referendum. There are also political reasons behind those votes. I remember that your reform was also rejected in 2016 by referendum. There are two cases. I do not believe that our fellow citizens are against the abolition of the CNEL, rather than the reduction in the number of Members. They do not want them to be asked a series of questions which they consider incomprehensible. So there is rejection and reform is rejected. For this reason, we have taken the path of surgical and punctual reforms, in which it was clear the will of what was being proposed and voted both here and, in the case, by the people. [[Applause from the L-SP-PSd'Az and M5S Groups]] Some say that the intervention is not of surgery, but even of microsurgery, because we intervened only on the number of parliamentarians. But do you think that there was not, even on the part of the members of the Commission themselves, the will - for example - to intervene on the active electorate of the Senate to make it truly universal suffrage? We all wanted to do it. Wouldn't anyone want to repeal the CNEL? We all wanted to do it. We have written that we would reduce the number of parliamentarians and have deliberately limited ourselves to this, within a context that is not exclusively of this single reform, because the individual reforms, if one wants, can see them in the government contract, which I know is not a document, but you can still consult [[Comments of Senator Pharaoh]] ; you can also remember reading what President Conte said, when he requested confidence, or the parliamentary accounts of the hearings of Minister Fraccaro. There you will find the individual pieces of the Lego with which you make the overall reform. And this is the state of the art. I have also heard many other beautiful things: we talked not only about reform, but also about the budget law, and much, about the decree simplifications and the numbers of the Mothers and the constituent Fathers. And that shocked me, because in the Constitution they never wrote the number of deputies and senators, which was born instead with Constitutional Law No. 2 of 1963. [[Applause from the L-SP-PSd'Az and M5S Groups.]] I do not think that the tyranny or the risk of democracy is at stake, which I have also heard of. You can say whatever you want, but I limit myself to the content of the law. Do you want to reduce the number of Members, yes or no? That is the first question. If I have to take what has been proposed and supported over the years, it seems to me that we want to reduce the number of Members. But saying yes or no can not be equivalent to what I have heard for two days, like: yes, but; we do the reduction tomorrow, or on the weekend, but there is my wife getting married; no, she has a communion and then there is the birthday of her uncle. [[Comments from the PD Group.]] No, now you have to target. Mr Marcucci (PD). Don't be a clown, talk about the Constitution! Mr Calderoli, rapporteur. Either you want to reduce it or you don't want to reduce it. The proposed reduction is 230 Members and 115 Senators. Then one may or may not agree on the reduction, may say that we have reduced them too much or that we have reduced them too little. It is absolutely there, but within this I want to carry out my own assessments, just as we did in the Commission, where there has been a number of problems that we have addressed and resolved. There was the Molise problem: one or two seats? We've decided for two, and we've solved the problem. There was the problem of the two autonomous provinces of Trento and Bolzano, which would have been penalized, with a heated comparison on the respect of the values contained in measure 111 of the so-called South Tyrol package, and we solved it. We had the problem of the right number with respect to the minimum that each Region should have and debated starting from the seven of the current Constitution, but there were those who proposed six, five, four; then we identified the three as the most balanced number, because unfortunately we have Regions that range from just over 300,000 inhabitants to ten million and succeed in guaranteeing to all a balance, respecting the fact that the Senate is elected on a regional basis, has not been absolutely easy. We think it is the best possible solution - or the least worst - but mathematics is such and this is a 36.5 percent reduction. I would also like to say this with respect to those of the opposition who said that we have solved this and this other problem, but only those of the elected abroad remain. With the proposed number of senators, equal to 200, I believe that there will be many problems in reorganizing the Commissions and, therefore, perhaps another time we will have to put our hands back, if this reform were finally passed, also to the Rules of Procedure of the Senate. ParlaMint-IT_2019-02-06-LEG18-Senato-sed-88.ana.u222	Mr President, a number of sub-amendments have been tabled to Amendment 2.100, tabled by the rapporteur, Senator Calderoli, who, when considering the linguistic minorities - a subject which he has not dealt with very much - has reconfected the number of Members of Parliament with regard to the autonomous provinces of Trento and Bolzano. Mr President, I have read some of the figures relating to the proportion of senators and voters with regard to the Upper Houses, and we are talking about the Senate. In the United Kingdom, the number of inhabitants per member is 83,000 and the number of members per 100,000 inhabitants is 1.2. In France, it is 0.5; in Italy - currently - 0.5; in Spain, 0.6. With the new proposal, the coefficient in Italy would be 0.3. Again, we have tried to do something that is anchored in a clearer European framework. I do not read the data from other countries that I hope will stay far enough away from us. I do not give you the example of Slovenia, Ireland and Austria. I have reconsidered the coefficient because - we will see it later, looking at the Senate Act 881, concerning the adaptation of the electoral law to the new numbers, resulting from the reduction of 36% of the number of parliamentarians - we will have great, but great problems of representation of the territories, with very large colleges that - I say also to the Minister - will create further problems of separation between representatives and representatives. In fact, if already with the current law it is very difficult to be able to understand which candidates are and to have a relationship with them in the very large single-member colleges, with such reductions and with the consequent adaptation of the electoral law, this relationship will be relaxed even more. Here too we have done a job of trying to attest to ourselves on an even lower European average. For the House we have proposed a coefficient of 0.8, (in Germany it is 0.9), with a mediation with respect to the proposal of 0.7, and also for the Senate we present a proposal of 265 senators, with a coefficient of 0.4. ParlaMint-IT_2019-02-06-LEG18-Senato-sed-88.ana.u231	I have told you that there is a sensitive issue concerning linguistic minorities, which, as you know, are recognised by our Constitution, in which they have a considerable weight. The Sardinian senators are not in the Chamber, except perhaps one (and at this moment I am standing as Uras even if he has not been re-elected). We have come to the point, as at the end of the previous legislature, that we did not even want to ratify a treaty that recognizes and gives strength to linguistic minorities, that in our country are not only, with all the respect of the SVP, the German minority. In this country, in fact, there are several border areas. For example, the issue of the Slovenian minority is very serious and very delicate also for the history of this country and those borders, and you know it perfectly well. In Friuli-Venezia Giulia there is an issue that historically also affects other components but in general in our country there are linguistic minorities in very large areas. In the past legislature we had problems with the Ladins because we have now settled the issue with the three plus three senators for the Autonomous Provinces but it is not said that the Ladina minority within that scheme is represented and this also strictly concerns South Tyrol and its border with the Autonomous Province of Trento. There is also a language that is not actually recognized: the Sardinian language. Shall we talk about Catalan? The Catalan language not only survives but is also used in some universities; it has a strong identity and does not have any forms of protection although Sardinia is an autonomous region with a special statute, not even with regard to the rules relating to RAI and communication, not to mention the school. I see Senator Buccarella here and he knows exactly what I mean: in Puglia there is a geographical area called Grecìa where the ancient griko is still spoken. All these forms do not have, I do not say a protection like the one we are facing within the representation, but not even a minimum of protection to guarantee the cultural and linguistic biodiversity of this country. Mr President, ladies and gentlemen, on the sidelines of today's parliamentary work, I would like to draw the attention of this House to an event which I believe should be strongly stigmatised. The principle of subsidiarity requires us full respect for the decision-making autonomy of the Macerata administration, but with regard to the content and decisions taken by the Municipality of Macerata, using that autonomy, it is legitimate, as well as necessary, to disagree and express a moral condemnation. A fundamental principle of the UN Convention on the Rights of Childhood and Adolescence is what a teenager - and at eighteen years of age still is - has the right to life, survival and development: exactly what with horrible ferocity was denied to Pamela Mastropietro. As if that were not enough, a few days ago the mayor of Macerata denied her the right to survive in the collective memory of the community that was the scene of her death. It's easy to see which party the mayor of Macerata belongs to, the same mayor who didn't judge the murderers but the girl's family, which I think is quite shameful. President, this case becomes emblematic of a way of thinking typical of the left: for them there are dead series A and dead series B. The only ones they want to remember are those who allow them to make propaganda, while those who have had the misfortune to die under politically incorrect circumstances are condemned to oblivion and forgetfulness. But we, President, must not forget. [[Applause from the L-SP-PSd'Az and M5S Groups.]]

ParlaMint-IT_2019-02-06-LEG18-Senato-sed-88.ana.u242	CORBETTA (M5S). Mr President, every year in Italy we have 90,000 deaths caused by air pollution. The health effects are very serious and affect especially the weakest sections of the population, such as the elderly and children. Traffic is one of the main drivers of this dramatic emergency. It is estimated that around 600 deaths in Milan are due to the high levels of nitrogen oxides that are mainly emitted by diesel engines. Incisive action is needed to reduce private traffic and increase public transport. In this scenario, the innovations that are expected in the public transport of Milan are of fundamental importance and you cannot afford to miss. The tariff integration of local public transport - for which the territorial groups of the 5 Star Movement have collected thousands of signatures in recent years - would be good news, because it would allow citizens to move more easily between Milan and hinterland. On the other hand, the statements made by the mayor of Milan, Beppe Sala, of binding the extension of subway 5 in Monza to the increase of ATM tickets and subscriptions, the Milanese transport company, are bad news. First of all, those of the Monza metro and ATM fares are two separate issues and they must remain so. It is unacceptable for the Mayor Sala to cross over on the Monza subway, which he himself pushed until yesterday. It is a project that would alleviate huge traffic and pollution problems, which the territory has been waiting for decades and which our Government has financed with as much as EUR 900 million in the budget law. The increase in the urban ticket from 1.5 to 2 euros is a short-sighted choice, which discourages the use of public transport in the city, instead of facilitating it. Perhaps the mayor of Milan does not understand that public transport is not only an essential service for the citizens who use it, but it is also and above all a system of vital importance for the whole city. What would Milan be without metro, trams and buses? It would be chaos and we see it every time there is the strike of means. It is clear that the citizen taking public transport helps to limit traffic and pollution for the benefit of all. Those who take the subway and buses are also good for those who, by choice or by necessity, use the private car. Using public transport is therefore a virtuous choice, which a mayor should encourage and reward; even more so the mayor of the richest city in Italy should do so: increasing urban rates is a suicidal choice, which Milan cannot afford. [[Applause from the M5S Group.]]
ParlaMint-IT_2019-02-06-LEG18-Senato-sed-88.ana.u243	I agree with your report, for a high speed Milan-Monza: this seems to me to have been the case.
